Both the length and intensity of the feedback mechanism (how much history is considered and how strongly it affects payoffs) increase the fraction of cooperators in the population. The model does not include punishment, reward, or sanctions, and does not report efficiency or group payoff as outcomes. The results are based on simulations of cooperation density and strategy dynamics.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or report efficiency or payoff-based outcomes. Its findings on endogenous feedback and nonlinear payoffs may be contextually relevant for understanding mechanisms that promote cooperation, but are not directly transferable to the prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency.

- source: Li, Y. P., Hu, X. Y., Jin, X., Zhang, H. Z., Yang, J. J., & Wang, Z. (2025). Environmental information perception enhances cooperation in stochastic public goods games via Q-learning. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in a stochastic PGG with environmental feedback, the ability of individuals to perceive environmental state information significantly promotes the emergence and stability of cooperation. Specifically, the critical synergy factor (r) required for cooperation to emerge is lower when individuals can perceive the environment, compared to both a static environment and a dynamic environment that is not perceived. The study uses Q-learning agents on spatial networks and explores a variety of model extensions (update rules, neighborhood size, network topology, etc.), consistently finding that environmental information perception lowers the cooperation threshold. However, the paper does not analyze or report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, and does not include any punishment or sanction mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes. Its findings are adjacent to the prediction task, as they show that environmental information perception (not punishment) can promote cooperation (not efficiency) in stochastic PGGs. The results may be useful for understanding how information and feedback mechanisms affect cooperation thresholds, but they do not inform the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff. For the prediction task, this paper is not directly informative.

- source: Wang, X. W., Jiang, L. L., Nie, S., & Wang, B. H. (2015). Uncovering cooperative behaviors with sparse historical behavior data in the spatial games. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper uses agent-based simulations to study how conformity-driven behavior affects cooperation in public goods games with binary heterogeneous endowments (rich and poor). The main finding is a 'double-edged sword' effect: when the multiplication factor (synergy of cooperation) is high, conformity enhances cooperation by strengthening network reciprocity; when the multiplication factor is low, conformity inhibits cooperation, especially among the poor, by allowing defection to infiltrate cooperative clusters. These effects are robust to variations in conformity strength, the proportion of rich, and the extension to multiplayer PGGs. However, the study only reports on cooperation rates, not on efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. Its findings on conformity and cooperation rates in heterogeneous endowment settings may be contextually informative for understanding behavioral dynamics in PGGs, but are not directly transferable to efficiency or payoff outcomes, nor to the effects of punishment. Thus, it should not be used as primary evidence for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency.

- source: Liu, A. Z., Zhang, Y. L., Chen, X. J., & Sun, C. Y. (2017). Randomness and diversity matter in the maintenance of the public resources. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that both greater diversity (more available contribution levels) and greater randomness (higher mutation, smaller population, weaker selection) increase the average investment in the public goods game. Under weak selection, average investment is high and insensitive to diversity or randomness. Under strong selection, average investment is low and insensitive to diversity or population size, but increases with mutation. Under intermediate selection, average investment increases with diversity and mutation, and decreases with population size. No payoff or efficiency outcomes are reported, and no punishment or reward mechanisms are present.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not inform prediction of efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or report efficiency or payoff outcomes. It is only relevant for understanding how diversity in contribution options and evolutionary randomness affect average investment in the absence of enforcement mechanisms. It provides no evidence about the effect of punishment or sanctions on efficiency.

- source: Flores, L. S., Vainstein, M. H., Fernandes, H. C. M., & Amaral, M. A. (2023). Heterogeneous contributions can jeopardize cooperation in the public goods game. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The study demonstrates that in spatial PGGs with imitation dynamics, there is an optimal homogeneous contribution value that maximizes cooperation (measured as cooperator density) for a given lattice structure. Introducing heterogeneity in contributions (i.e., allowing cooperators to contribute different amounts) can reduce the overall level of cooperation compared to this optimum, due to parasitic interactions where higher-contribution cooperators are exploited by lower-contribution ones. The effect of heterogeneity depends on the lattice structure: on square lattices, heterogeneity is detrimental to cooperation at the optimum, while on triangular lattices (with clustering), the effect is less negative. The main dependent variable is the equilibrium density of cooperators; efficiency or group payoff is not directly measured.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms. Its findings are relevant for understanding how heterogeneity in contributions (a form of player heterogeneity) affects cooperation rates, but not efficiency or payoff outcomes. The results may be useful for contextualizing the impact of contribution heterogeneity as a moderator, but not for the core prediction task involving punishment. The absence of punishment means this paper should not be used as primary evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Gao, B., Li, X., Hou, S. X., Jia, D. Y., & Du, M. J. (2019). Resolving public goods dilemma by giving the poor more support. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that narrowing the income gap among group members (negative alpha, i.e., redistributive policies favoring the poor) increases the frequency of cooperation in a spatial public goods game, while increasing income inequality (positive alpha) reduces cooperation. The model is simulated on a lattice, and the main outcome is the proportion of cooperators under different redistribution regimes. No punishment, reward, or sanctioning mechanisms are present, and no efficiency or group payoff outcomes are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or report efficiency or payoff outcomes. Its findings about redistribution and cooperation rates may be of indirect interest for understanding how inequality and redistribution affect cooperation, but it is not relevant for the core prediction task regarding punishment and efficiency.

- source: Wang, Y. F., Vasilakos, A. V., & Ma, J. H. (2015). VPEF: A Simple and Effective Incentive Mechanism in Community-Based Autonomous Networks. *Ieee Transactions on Network and Service Management*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: VPEF, an incentive mechanism based on voluntary participation, round-based entry fees, and voluntary costly punishment, leads to high efficiency in community-based collaborative networks. The system spends most of its time in the punisher state, where all participants contribute and punish defectors, minimizing efficiency loss. The efficiency loss is analytically shown to be 4/(8+M), which becomes negligible for large communities. Simulations confirm the theoretical predictions. The mechanism is robust to changes in group size, entry fee (as long as it is not too high), and multiplexing/diversity factor. The model assumes strong selection (users imitate higher-payoff strategies) and does not require tracking long-term behavior or permanent exclusion.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct theoretical and simulation-based evidence that enabling voluntary costly punishment in a public-goods-like environment with voluntary participation and a small entry fee can dramatically increase efficiency, driving the system to near-full cooperation (punisher state) and minimizing efficiency loss. The efficiency loss formula (4/(8+M)) can be used to predict the efficiency of the punishment-enabled game, given the number of users. The findings are robust across a range of parameter values, provided the multiplexing/diversity factor is above 1 and defectors can be reliably detected. The model is most applicable to dynamic, anonymous, and voluntary participation settings, and less so to settings with continuous contribution levels or where punishment/reward is not voluntary.

- source: Kol'veková, G., Raisová, M., Zoricak, M., & Gazda, V. (2021). Endogenous Shared Punishment Model in Threshold Public Goods Games. *Computational Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The endogenous shared punishment model, where agents vote on the level and cost of punishment, achieves higher mean payoffs (efficiency) than both the baseline (no punishment) and the cooperation (full punishment) models. In the standard scenario (group size 4, MPCR 0.5, threshold 60, endowment 30), the endogenous shared punishment model yields a weighted mean reward of 40.09, compared to 33.42 for the baseline and 35.04 for the cooperation model. The provision rate is 73.8% for the endogenous shared punishment model, 24.7% for the baseline, and 98.2% for the cooperation model. The endogenous shared punishment model achieves this efficiency at a very low cost of punishment, as pro-punishment votes and punishment costs decline over time as cooperation stabilizes. Adding 'activists' (altruistic contributors) further increases provision rates and mean payoffs. The model's results are robust to variations in group size, threshold, MPCR, and learning parameters.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing a low-cost, endogenous, shared punishment mechanism in threshold public goods games can substantially increase efficiency (mean group payoff) compared to both no-punishment and full-punishment regimes. The efficiency gains are achieved even when the provision rate is not maximized, due to the low cost of punishment and the stabilization of cooperation. The findings are directly relevant for predicting the effect of enabling punishment in TPGG-like environments, especially when punishment is endogenous, low-cost, and shared. The results are most applicable to games with group size 4, MPCR 0.5, and threshold 60, but the model explores a range of parameters. The paper's agent-based approach and focus on payoff outcomes make it highly informative for the prediction task.

- source: Yamamoto, H., & Okada, I. (2016). How to keep punishment to maintain cooperation: Introducing social vaccine. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in the meta-norms game, costly punishment and meta-punishment alone are insufficient to robustly maintain cooperation and high group efficiency, due to the vulnerability to second-order free-riding. However, the coercive introduction of a small number of always-defecting agents (the 'social vaccine') stabilizes the punishment system, prevents the drift toward non-punishing cooperators, and maintains a highly cooperative regime. This results in average payoffs for the group that are close to those of a fully cooperative group, even in parameter regions where cooperation would otherwise collapse. The effect is robust to changes in population size, temptation to defect, and punishment cost.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that, in public-goods-like environments with meta-norms (second-order punishment), the presence of a small number of persistent defectors can dramatically increase the efficiency of the punishment regime, maintaining high group payoffs where they would otherwise collapse. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the stability and efficiency of punishment-enabled games may depend not only on the formal punishment parameters but also on the presence of persistent norm violators. In the absence of such 'social vaccine' agents, efficiency gains from punishment may be fragile or short-lived, especially in longer games or with low punishment costs. The findings are most directly relevant to games with meta-punishment and compulsory participation, and may be less directly transferable to standard linear PGGs without meta-norms.

- source: Jiang, L. L., Chen, Z., Perc, M., Wang, Z., Kurths, J., & Moreno, Y. (2023). Deterrence through punishment can resolve collective risk dilemmas in carbon emission games. *Chaos*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a lab experiment simulating a collective-risk public goods game (carbon emission game), costly punishment of free-riders (with fines imposed probabilistically and costs shared by all group members) increases the probability that groups reach the collective target, but only when the punishment risk is high (p > 0.8). At low punishment risk, deterrence is weak, and group failure rates remain high. Larger groups are less likely to succeed at any given punishment risk, requiring higher punishment risk for similar deterrence. The introduction of punishment shifts most players to fair-share contributions, reducing both free-riding and maximal altruism. The effect of punishment on group success (a proxy for efficiency) is robust to different loss probabilities. The study does not report efficiency as a ratio of actual to maximum possible group payoff, but the probability of reaching the target and average investment are closely related and show a positive effect of punishment when sufficiently strong.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in collective-risk public goods games with a threshold target and probabilistic costly punishment, enabling punishment increases group success rates (and thus efficiency) only when the punishment risk is high. The effect is moderated by group size (larger groups require higher punishment risk) and is robust to different loss probabilities. For prediction, the presence and strength of punishment (punishment risk, fine size, cost structure), group size, and the absence of communication are key moderators. The control (no-punishment) condition yields low group success rates, especially as group size increases. The findings support a more positive effect of punishment on efficiency when punishment is strong and credible, but little or no effect when punishment is weak or infrequent.

- source: De Silva, H., & Sigmund, K. (2009). Public Good Games with Incentives: The Role of Reputation. *Games, Groups, and the Global Good*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in public goods games with punishment, cooperation and punishment do not persist in the long run under strict anonymity; the population converges to asocial strategies. Introducing reputation—where players can learn about others' punishment behavior—creates bistability: both full cooperation with punishment and full defection without punishment can be stable. The presence of errors (occasional failures to cooperate) is necessary for reputation to be informative and for punishment to have an effect. The model is primarily theoretical and does not provide explicit efficiency or group payoff results, but the implication is that reputation can enable punishment to support higher cooperation and, by extension, higher efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the importance of reputation and information flow in enabling punishment to sustain cooperation in public goods games. In the absence of reputation, punishment does not improve efficiency; with reputation, punishment can support high cooperation, but outcomes depend on initial conditions and parameters. For prediction tasks, the presence or absence of reputation mechanisms (e.g., information about others' punishment behavior) is a critical moderator of punishment's effect on efficiency. However, the paper does not provide quantitative efficiency outcomes, so its support is indirect and mechanistic rather than predictive.

- source: Zhang, C. Y., Liu, Z. X., Sun, Q. L., & Chen, Z. Q. (2015). Altruism in multiplayer snowdrift games with threshold and punishment. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Theoretical results show that introducing punishment for defectors in threshold N-person snowdrift games increases the equilibrium fraction of cooperators, especially when punishment is always applied regardless of group success. Stronger punishment and higher cooperation thresholds further increase cooperation. However, the model does not include costs for administering punishment, and does not report group efficiency or total payoff. The findings are relevant for understanding how punishment rules and thresholds affect cooperation, but do not directly inform the effect of punishment on group efficiency or welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that punishment increases cooperation in threshold public goods-like games, especially when punishment is always applied and is strong. However, because the model assumes costless punishment and does not report group efficiency or total payoff, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings are most useful for understanding how punishment regime, threshold, and punishment strength affect cooperation rates, which may correlate with efficiency in some settings, but the lack of punishment cost and direct efficiency outcomes limits predictive value for efficiency.

- source: Chung, N. N., Chew, L. Y., & Lai, C. H. (2013). Influence of network structure on cooperative dynamics in coupled socio-ecological systems. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study extends a previous analytical model of social sanction (ostracism) in common pool resource extraction to populations with complex network structures. It finds that, contrary to some prior work, cooperation and resource sustainability are promoted by higher network connectivity when social sanction is present. In networks with fewer social ties, defectors can cluster and avoid sanction, leading to lower cooperation and lower equilibrium resource levels. The model shows that the equilibrium resource level (a proxy for group efficiency) is higher in well-mixed or highly connected networks, and lower in sparsely connected ones. The findings are robust across different network topologies (random, scale-free, community-structured) and network sizes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that, in public-goods-like common pool resource games with social sanction (ostracism), increasing the average number of social ties (network degree) increases the effectiveness of punishment in promoting cooperation and sustaining higher resource levels (efficiency). For the downstream prediction task, this suggests that, all else equal, games with higher player connectivity or more opportunities for sanctioning will see a greater positive effect of punishment on efficiency. The paper does not provide direct experimental data, but its results are highly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment in networked or structured populations.

- source: Hua, S. J., & Liu, L. J. (2023). Governance of risky public goods under the threat of ostracism. *Physica D-nonlinear Phenomena*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a collective risk dilemma (CRD) model, local exclusion (ostracism) is more effective than local punishment in promoting group achievement (i.e., the fraction of groups that reach the collective target), especially at low risk levels. Local sanctioning institutions (both punishment and exclusion) are more effective than global ones. The advantage of exclusion over punishment is most pronounced when the risk of collective failure is low. When risk is high, both exclusion and punishment lead to high group achievement. The results are based on theoretical analysis and simulation of evolutionary dynamics, not on direct measurement of efficiency or group payoff, but group achievement is a closely related outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that, in threshold public goods environments with risk of collective loss (CRD), enabling local punishment or exclusion mechanisms increases group achievement (a proxy for efficiency) compared to no sanctions, with local exclusion (ostracism) being especially effective at low risk. The results suggest that, for similar game designs, enabling punishment (especially local, exclusionary forms) is likely to increase efficiency or group success, particularly when the risk of failure is not too high. However, the findings are based on group achievement rather than explicit efficiency ratios, and are derived from theoretical models rather than empirical data. The model parameters (e.g., group size, cost of exclusion, risk level) are specified, allowing some mapping to prediction dimensions.

- source: Noussair, C. N., van Soest, D., & Stoop, J. (2015). Punishment, reward, and cooperation in a framed field experiment. *Social Choice and Welfare*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that introducing punishment or reward opportunities does not increase cooperation compared to baseline: average catch and effort are statistically indistinguishable across treatments. Punishment is directed at non-cooperators, but does not induce greater cooperation in subsequent periods. Reward is used more frequently than punishment, but is not targeted based on recipient cooperation. The results suggest that, in this field setting, peer punishment and reward are ineffective at increasing cooperation, in contrast to many laboratory PGG studies. No direct measures of group efficiency or total payoff are reported, but the behavioral proxies (catch and effort) do not improve with punishment or reward.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a field setting with a common-pool resource framed as a social dilemma, enabling peer punishment (and reward) does not increase cooperation or its behavioral proxies, compared to a baseline with no sanctions. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the positive effect of punishment on efficiency observed in many lab PGGs may not generalize to field environments with similar incentive structures but different context, framing, or real-world constraints. However, because the paper does not report explicit efficiency or group payoff outcomes, its direct value for predicting efficiency is limited; it is most informative as a caution about generalizability and as evidence that punishment may not always improve group outcomes, even when the game structure is similar to a PGG.

- source: Esmaeili, P., Makui, A., Seyedhosseini, S. M., & Ghousi, R. (2022). The effect of probabilistic incentives to promote cooperation during the pandemics using simulation of multi-agent evolutionary game. *International Journal of Industrial Engineering Computations*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that probabilistic third-party incentives (reward and punishment) can trigger abrupt transitions from low to high cooperation in a networked volunteer dilemma game. Pure punishment is more effective than pure reward, but a combination of both (with a higher share for reward) is optimal for promoting cooperation at lower monitoring costs. The main reported outcome is the cooperation rate, not efficiency or group payoff. The findings are based on agent-based simulations and catastrophe theory analysis, not empirical data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the prediction task by showing that the presence and probability of punishment (and reward) can cause sharp increases in cooperation rates in volunteer dilemma games. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so its direct value for predicting efficiency outcomes is limited. The results suggest that punishment (especially when combined with reward) can be a powerful lever for increasing cooperation, which may translate to higher efficiency in similar public goods settings, but this is not directly demonstrated. The paper is most useful for understanding the qualitative impact of incentive mechanisms and the potential for nonlinear effects, rather than for quantitative prediction of efficiency.

- source: Li, Q. R., Li, Y. Q., Li, K., Chen, L., Zheng, Q., & Chen, K. (2020). Study on the influence of subjective well-being on travel mode selection. *Physics Letters A*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing both punishment and reward mechanisms, based on subjective well-being thresholds, greatly increases the proportion of travelers choosing the cooperative (low-carbon) option. Punishment is found to be more effective than reward in stabilizing high levels of cooperation. The ease of triggering incentives (by narrowing the threshold interval) further boosts cooperation. However, the study does not report on group payoff, efficiency, or welfare; all results are in terms of cooperation rates.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the prediction task by showing that punishment (and to a lesser extent, reward) mechanisms can substantially increase cooperation rates in a PGG-like setting. However, because the model and results focus on cooperation rates rather than efficiency or group payoff, the findings should be used with caution for predicting efficiency outcomes. The model structure and parameterization (e.g., cost and effectiveness of punishment/reward) are informative for understanding how design dimensions might affect cooperation, but not directly efficiency.

- source: Shutters, S. T. (2012). Punishment Leads to Cooperative Behavior in Structured Societies. *Evolutionary Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that in agent-based simulations of a continuous prisoner's dilemma, cooperation (measured as mean contribution) does not evolve in the absence of both punishment and social structure. When only one of these is present, cooperation also does not evolve. However, when both social structure (local interactions on networks) and the option for costly punishment are present, populations can evolve to high levels of cooperation, depending on the strength of punishment and the network structure. The transition from defection to cooperation requires stronger punishment as network density increases. Scale-free networks are an exception, showing little or no response to punishment. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff as an outcome, so direct implications for efficiency are not available.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the presence of both local social structure and effective punishment mechanisms is necessary for the evolution of high cooperation rates in continuous-contribution social dilemmas. However, because the outcome is mean contribution rather than efficiency or group payoff, the findings must be interpreted as indirect evidence for efficiency effects. The results suggest that, in structured populations (except scale-free networks), enabling punishment can dramatically increase cooperation rates, which would likely increase efficiency, but the paper does not quantify this directly. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the idea that punishment is only effective in structured (non-well-mixed) populations, and that the effectiveness of punishment depends on network density and punishment strength.

- source: Prietula, M. J., & Conway, D. (2009). The evolution of metanorms: quis custodiet ipsos custodes?. *Computational and Mathematical Organization Theory*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in agent-based simulations of a public-goods-like dilemma, the addition of metanorms (punishing those who do not punish defectors) is necessary for the emergence of a behavioral norm against defection. When only first-order punishment is available, defection dominates. When metanorms are present, defection rates drop and punishment rates rise, indicating norm emergence. Decoupling the strategies for punishing defectors and non-punishers does not prevent norm emergence and can reduce the overall level of punishment required. In group-structured games with unequal power, metanorms alone do not produce a norm against defection; only with decoupled sanctioning structures does the strong group enforce a norm against the weak group's defection, leading to group dominance rather than universal cooperation. The outcomes are reported in terms of behavioral strategy values (boldness, vengefulness), not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the prediction task by showing that the presence of punishment and especially metanorms (second-order punishment) can dramatically reduce defection rates in public-goods-like environments, even when punishment is costly. However, the outcomes are reported in terms of behavioral measures (defection, punishment) rather than efficiency or group payoff, so direct quantitative prediction of efficiency is not possible. The findings suggest that enabling punishment (especially with metanorms) is likely to increase efficiency in analogous empirical games, but the magnitude and exact efficiency outcomes are not reported. The paper also highlights that group structure and the sophistication of the punishment mechanism (e.g., decoupling punishment types) can moderate the effect, sometimes leading to group dominance rather than overall cooperation.

- source: Wang, Z., Li, R. D., Jin, X., & Ding, H. (2023). Emergence of Social Norms in Metanorms Game With High-Order Interaction Topology. *Ieee Transactions on Computational Social Systems*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in metanorm games on uniform random hypergraphs (URH), increasing group size (G) promotes the emergence of social norms, leading to higher cooperation rates and more agents willing to punish defectors. Network connectivity (number of groups) does not affect norm emergence. On heterogeneous hypergraphs (HRH), standard BV-learning fails to establish norms for 'leaver' agents, but a new dynamic relevance BV-learning algorithm enables both hub and leaver agents to establish norms and cooperate. Real-world hypergraph data (coauthorship networks) show similar patterns. The main findings are about the conditions for norm emergence and high cooperation, not about efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is informative for predicting the effect of punishment on cooperation and norm compliance in public-goods-like games with high-order (group) interactions, especially regarding the role of group size and network structure. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. The results suggest that larger group size and effective learning algorithms for punishment/metapunishment can promote cooperation and norm compliance, which may be associated with higher efficiency in related settings, but this is not directly measured.

- source: Van Cleve, J. (2016). Cooperation, conformity, and the coevolutionary problem of trait associations. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the coevolution of cooperation, punishment, and conformist bias in a metapopulation model requires a strong initial within-group association (high F_ST) between these traits. If this threshold is not met, cooperation and punishment do not evolve to high frequency, and conformist bias does not stabilize cooperation. The threshold is around F_ST = 0.2 for large groups. The model assumes a public-goods-type group interaction with costly cooperation and punishment, and group competition is based on accumulated resources (which are higher with more cooperation). However, the paper does not directly report efficiency or group payoff as an outcome, focusing instead on the evolutionary dynamics of the traits.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the prediction task: it suggests that the effectiveness of punishment in promoting cooperation (and thus, by implication, efficiency) in public-goods-like environments depends critically on initial population structure (within-group association). In well-mixed or unstructured populations, punishment may not evolve or persist, and thus may not improve efficiency. However, the paper does not provide direct quantitative predictions about efficiency or group payoff, nor does it report the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in a controlled game setting. Its findings are most relevant for understanding the conditions under which punishment can support cooperation in evolutionary or long-run settings, rather than for predicting short-run efficiency changes in experimental games.

- source: Cheng, H. H., Meng, X. Z., Hayat, T., & Hobiny, A. (2023). Multistability and bifurcation analysis for a three-strategy game system with public goods feedback and discrete delays. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that both punishment and reward can promote the survival of cooperators and the abundance of public goods, but only when their intensities are sufficiently high. Moderate reward and severe punishment are most conducive to cooperation. Excessively high reward does not always further increase cooperation. The cost of reward has a stronger negative effect on cooperation than the cost of punishment. Initial conditions and time delays can lead to multistability, oscillations, or extinction of cooperation. Large delays are detrimental to cooperation and can lead to the extinction of cooperators. The model highlights the complex interplay between feedback, incentives, and dynamic stability in public goods environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and numerical evidence that the effect of punishment on cooperation and public good abundance is positive when punishment is sufficiently strong and not too costly, especially when combined with moderate reward. However, because the model does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, its findings should be interpreted as supporting the idea that punishment can increase cooperation and public good provision under certain parameter regimes, but not as direct evidence for efficiency gains. The results suggest that the effect of punishment is sensitive to the costs of incentives, the presence of reward, and dynamic factors such as delays and initial conditions. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the expectation that punishment can improve group outcomes in public-goods-like settings, but the mapping to efficiency is indirect.

- source: Yan, Z. Y., Zhao, H., Liang, S., Li, L., & Song, Y. J. (2024). Inter-layer feedback mechanism with reinforcement learning boosts the evolution of cooperation in multilayer network. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study demonstrates that adaptive punishment mechanisms, when combined with reinforcement learning in hypergraph-structured PGGs, substantially increase the fraction of cooperators. Key findings include: (1) higher punishment probability and intensity promote cooperation, (2) moderate punishment costs are optimal for sustaining cooperation, (3) smaller group sizes (hyperedges) facilitate earlier and higher cooperation, and (4) higher learning rates and discount factors in Q-learning further enhance cooperation. However, all results are reported in terms of cooperation rates, not group efficiency or total payoff. The model is theoretical and simulation-based, not empirical.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that adaptive punishment mechanisms can increase cooperation rates in PGG-like environments, especially when punishment is likely, strong, and not too costly, and when group sizes are small. However, because the paper does not report efficiency, welfare, or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The results are most useful for predicting how design dimensions (punishment cost, intensity, group size, etc.) affect cooperation rates, which may correlate with efficiency but are not equivalent. For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides indirect, mechanistic support for the positive effect of punishment on cooperation, but not direct evidence for efficiency outcomes.

- source: Kurokawa, S. (2019). Three-player repeated games with an opt-out option. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in a three-player repeated public goods game with an opt-out option (no explicit punishment), both tolerant and intolerant cooperators can survive, but their coexistence is not an evolutionary outcome. The equilibrium frequency of cooperators (and thus average payoff) increases with the benefit-to-cost ratio and decreases with the probability of group dissolution. The model shows that (partially) cooperative stable equilibria can exist, but the all-defector equilibrium is always asymptotically stable, making the initial evolution of cooperation difficult without additional mechanisms. The paper does not study punishment or sanctioning, so it does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is relevant for understanding the effect of voluntary partner refusal (opt-out) on cooperation and efficiency in small-group public goods games, but it does not address punishment or sanctioning. It provides analytic results on how group size, benefit-to-cost ratio, and group dissolution probability affect equilibrium cooperation and payoffs. However, it does not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment, as no punishment mechanism is present. Its results may be useful as a baseline for comparison to punishment-enabled games, especially regarding the efficiency achievable through partner choice alone.

- source: Yang, L. H., Xu, Z. J., Zhang, L. Z., & Yang, D. X. (2018). Strategy intervention in spatial voluntary public goods games. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: This simulation study finds that a centralized intervention mechanism (distinct from peer or pool punishment) can increase the average total payoff of the group and reduce income inequality, provided the intervention is sufficiently strong (i.e., the product of intervention degree and intensity exceeds a threshold). Weak intervention can actually reduce cooperation and group payoff compared to no intervention. The model provides explicit parameter ranges for when full cooperation and maximum efficiency are achieved. The intervention cost is shared by all, and the mechanism is implemented by a central authority, not by individuals punishing each other.
  prediction_guidance: For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that centralized, institutionally-enforced interventions (akin to pool punishment but implemented as forced contributions rather than direct payoff reduction) can increase efficiency in spatial voluntary PGGs, but only if the intervention is strong enough. The effect is non-monotonic: weak intervention can be counterproductive. The findings are most relevant for settings with voluntary participation, spatial structure, and centralized enforcement. The explicit mapping between intervention parameters and efficiency outcomes can inform predictions about the efficiency impact of institutional punishment-like mechanisms, especially when the control (no intervention) efficiency is low. However, the mechanism is not standard punishment, so direct transfer to peer punishment settings should be cautious.

- source: Castro, L., & Toro, M. A. (2008). Iterated prisoner's dilemma in an asocial world dominated by loners, not by defectors. *Theoretical Population Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated N-person Prisoner's Dilemma games, the presence of a 'loner' strategy (voluntary opt-out for a fixed payoff) changes the evolutionary dynamics: loners can displace defectors, and conditional cooperators (TFT0) can invade a population of loners if the benefit of cooperation is high enough and the number of repeated interactions is sufficient. The model demonstrates that cooperation can be established and maintained in sizable groups without the need for explicit punishment, provided that individuals can opt out. The paper does not model or test punishment directly, but discusses its potential role as an additional mechanism for sustaining cooperation in large groups.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the prediction task by showing that voluntary participation (the loner strategy) can enable high-efficiency outcomes in repeated public-goods-like games, even without punishment. However, it does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency, as punishment is not modeled. The findings suggest that in environments where voluntary participation is possible, the need for punishment to sustain cooperation may be reduced. For prediction, this paper is most informative about the baseline efficiency in control games with voluntary participation, but not about the incremental effect of enabling punishment.

- source: Ding, R., Wang, X. J., Liu, Y., Zhao, J. H., & Gu, C. L. (2023). Evolutionary games with environmental feedbacks under an external incentive mechanism. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that external incentives (subsidies/carbon credits) can promote cooperation and improve environmental quality in a spatial public goods game with environmental feedback, but the effect is not always positive. Higher subsidies increase the competitiveness of cooperators (both those who reinvest in the public good and those who use credits for personal gain), but can also create conditions where defectors survive by exploiting improved environments, leading to coexistence or even dominance of defectors. The use of incentives to promote cooperation is thus sensitive to the balance between environmental repair and degradation rates, the structure of incentives, and the spatial dynamics of strategy clusters. The model does not include punishment or sanctions in the standard sense, but the 'second-order social dilemma' (where some cooperators act as free-riders on others' altruism) is analogous to the problem of sustaining costly punishment. The main outcomes are cooperation rates and environmental quality, with payoffs determined by these variables.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence for predicting the effect of external incentives (akin to rewards) on cooperation and group welfare in spatial public goods games with environmental feedback. It suggests that the effect of such mechanisms on efficiency or payoff is highly context-dependent: increasing the incentive can improve cooperation and welfare in some parameter regions, but can also reduce them in others due to complex spatial and strategic dynamics. The findings caution against assuming monotonic positive effects of incentives and highlight the importance of considering environmental feedbacks and the possibility of second-order free-riding. However, the model does not include punishment or sanctions as defined in the prediction task, so its direct applicability to punishment effects is limited. The results are most relevant for reward-based or subsidy-based interventions in spatial, environmentally-coupled PGGs.

- source: Becchetti, L., Pelligra, V., & Salustri, F. (2018). The impact of redistribution mechanisms in the vote with the wallet game: experimental results. *Social Choice and Welfare*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The introduction of an ex post redistribution mechanism (taxing defectors and subsidizing cooperators) in a repeated, all-or-nothing public good game significantly increases the share of cooperative choices compared to the baseline. In the baseline, cooperation decays over time, but with redistribution, cooperation is stabilized at a higher level. Framing the responsible product as socially beneficial also increases cooperation, but the effect of redistribution is robust across frames. The study does not report group efficiency or total payoff, so the effect on efficiency cannot be directly determined, but the increase in cooperation rates suggests likely higher group payoffs under redistribution.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that a centralized, ex post redistribution mechanism (akin to a tax/subsidy rather than peer punishment) can substantially increase cooperation rates in a repeated, all-or-nothing public good game with 10 players and no communication. While efficiency or group payoff is not directly reported, the structure of the payoffs and the increase in cooperation rates imply that group efficiency would also increase under the redistribution mechanism. The findings are most directly relevant for predicting the effect of centralized, automatic sanction/reward mechanisms (not peer punishment) in all-or-nothing, no-communication, 10-player repeated games with known rounds and MPCR of 0.3. The results are less directly applicable to standard peer punishment settings, but suggest that institutionalized redistribution can be highly effective at sustaining cooperation.

- source: Li, Y., Liu, X. S., Claussen, J. C., & Guo, W. L. (2015). Evolutionary dynamics for persistent cooperation in structured populations. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that persistent cooperators can invade and dominate structured populations (lattices) under more relaxed conditions than in well-mixed populations. The presence of persistent cooperators suppresses defectors and can lead to the dominance or coexistence of cooperative strategies, depending on the parameters (fraction of shared benefit, second cost, synergy factor). The evolutionary time to equilibrium is shorter in structured populations. The model shows that spatial structure facilitates the spread of persistent cooperation, and that the parameter region for cooperative dominance is larger than in well-mixed populations. However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, focusing instead on strategy frequencies and fixation probabilities.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that mechanisms analogous to costly punishment (here, persistent cooperation) can suppress defection and promote cooperation in structured populations, especially when the cost of retrieving one's own contribution is not too high and the fraction of shared benefit is not too large. However, because the model is not a standard punishment PGG and does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings suggest that spatial structure and the ability to pay a cost to counteract defectors can expand the parameter space where cooperation is stable, but the lack of payoff-based outcomes limits its direct applicability to efficiency prediction tasks.

- source: Min, Y., Du, Y. C., & Jin, C. (2018). The effect of link rewiring on a coevolutionary common pool resource game. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that allowing cooperators to rewire their links away from defectors (link rewiring) in a CPR game can prevent the collapse of cooperation and resource depletion, especially in sparsely connected networks and when resource inflow is high. The model uses a costless social ostracism mechanism (reputation-based exclusion) rather than costly punishment. The main reported outcomes are the fraction of cooperators and resource abundance, not group efficiency or total payoff. The results suggest that adaptive network structure (partner switching) can be a powerful mechanism for sustaining cooperation, but the effects depend on network density and ecological parameters.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of costly punishment on efficiency in public goods or CPR games. Instead, it analyzes a costless ostracism/reputation mechanism and the impact of adaptive network rewiring. While the findings are relevant for understanding how social structure and reputation can support cooperation, they do not inform predictions about the efficiency effects of enabling costly punishment. The model and results are adjacent to the prediction task, offering indirect insight into the role of network adaptation and costless sanctions, but not the direct impact of costly punishment on efficiency.

- source: Salomonsson, M. (2010). Group selection: The quest for social preferences. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that group selection can, under certain conditions, support the evolution of cooperation and altruism in public-goods-like and Prisoner's Dilemma games. Three main approaches are discussed: fixation (requiring isolated pure groups), assortative group formation (e.g., via conformity or signalling), and reproductive externalities (where group-level payoffs affect evolutionary success). The paper notes that group selection is more likely to support cooperation when migration is low, groups are small, or positive externalities can be internalized. It also reviews how cultural mechanisms (like conformity) and group competition can foster social preferences. However, the paper does not provide direct evidence or predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff, and only discusses punishment as a possible mechanism in the context of conformity and social preferences.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical context for how group structure, migration, and mechanisms like conformity or signalling can affect the evolution of cooperation in public-goods-like games. It is useful for understanding the broader conditions under which cooperation and social preferences may arise, but it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that group structure and the ability to internalize externalities are important moderators, but it does not inform the specific effect of punishment interventions in experimental public goods games.

- source: Zhang, J. L., & Zhang, C. Y. (2015). The evolution of altruism in spatial threshold public goods games via an insurance mechanism. *Journal of Statistical Mechanics-theory and Experiment*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=weak | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in threshold public goods games, offering insurance to cooperators (but not defectors) can promote the prevalence of cooperative strategies, especially when the compensation for failed games is high and the threshold for success is high. However, if defectors are also allowed to purchase insurance, insured defection can become dominant, leading to a decrease in cooperative behaviors. The network structure (random regular vs. scale-free) also moderates these effects. The main outcomes are the steady-state fractions of each strategy; efficiency or group payoff is not directly reported, but can be inferred from the modeled payoffs. The results suggest that insurance can substitute for some risk-mitigating effects of punishment, but the mechanism is distinct.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it studies insurance as a risk-mitigation mechanism rather than punishment or sanctions. However, it does provide indirect evidence about how risk-mitigation mechanisms (like insurance) can affect cooperation and, by implication, group welfare. The findings suggest that mechanisms which reduce the risk of loss for contributors (such as insurance) can promote cooperation, but if such mechanisms are also available to defectors, they may undermine cooperation. For prediction tasks, this paper is most relevant for understanding the effects of risk and insurance, not punishment. It does not provide direct parameterizations for the target game design dimensions related to punishment.

- source: Lee, J. H., Yamaguchi, R., Yokomizo, H., & Nakamaru, M. (2020). Preservation of the value of rice paddy fields: Investigating how to prevent farmers from abandoning the fields by means of evolutionary game theory. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that cooperation (maintenance effort) and thus group efficiency can be sustained when responsibility for collective action (maintenance) is local (among neighbors), but not when it is global (all players responsible for all facilities). Maintenance effort promotes cultivation effort, but not vice versa. Spatial structure and local interaction are key to sustaining group welfare. The model does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, so it does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings are directly relevant to predicting efficiency in nonlinear public goods games with spatial structure and local cooperation, but not to games with punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that local responsibility and spatial structure can sustain cooperation and group efficiency in nonlinear public goods games, but it does not inform the effect of punishment or sanctions. For prediction tasks involving punishment, this paper is not directly informative. However, it is highly informative for understanding the baseline efficiency of control games (without punishment) as a function of spatial structure, local vs. global responsibility, and ecological parameters. It can help predict when efficiency will be high or low in the absence of punishment, but not the effect of enabling punishment.

- source: Joshi, J., Brännström, & Dieckmann, U. (2020). Emergence of social inequality in the spatial harvesting of renewable public goods. *Plos Computational Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The model shows that when harvesting efficiency is low, all agents evolve to be sedentary and harvest at low rates, resulting in equitable but inefficient resource use. As harvesting efficiency increases, agents become mobile, and resource extraction approaches the maximum sustainable yield, achieving both efficiency and equity. Further increases in harvesting efficiency or reductions in dispersal cost lead to overexploitation and the emergence of social inequality, with a minority of mobile, overexploitative agents and a majority of frugal, sedentary agents. Rapid social learning (impatience) and local information (myopia) further aggravate inequality and inefficiency. The model identifies key moderators of efficiency and equity but does not include or analyze punishment or sanctioning mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for understanding how efficiency in spatial public goods–like environments depends on ecological and behavioral parameters such as harvesting efficiency, dispersal cost, consumer density, and information structure. However, it does not provide any evidence about the effects of punishment or sanctioning, as these mechanisms are absent from the model. Therefore, while the findings are useful for predicting efficiency in control (no-punishment) conditions and for understanding the role of mobility and social learning, the paper does not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to the introduction of punishment. It is best used for baseline efficiency estimation and for understanding moderators of overexploitation and inequality in the absence of sanctions.

- source: Song, Q. Q., Li, Z. P., Fu, C. H., & Wang, L. S. (2011). Optional contributions have positive effects for volunteering public goods games. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that introducing an option for low contributions in volunteering public goods games (VPG) increases the average payoff of the population (i.e., efficiency), compared to the standard VPG with only a single contribution level. This effect is observed in both well-mixed and spatially structured populations, and is especially pronounced when the multiplication factor (r) is above a threshold that depends weakly on group size. The mechanism is that the low-contribution option induces more players to cooperate, which in turn increases the number of high contributors as well, leading to higher overall payoffs. The results are based on theoretical analysis and extensive simulations of evolutionary dynamics.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in VPG-like public goods games, allowing optional (especially low) contributions can increase group efficiency compared to a single, higher contribution requirement. While the paper does not study punishment or sanctions, it is highly informative for predicting efficiency in games where the main design dimension is the contribution structure (e.g., all-or-nothing vs. continuous, or optional vs. required). The findings suggest that, for a given control efficiency, introducing more flexible contribution options (even without punishment) can increase efficiency, especially when the MPCR is above a moderate threshold. However, the paper does not address the effects of punishment, so it is not directly informative for the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Quan, J., Zhang, X. M., Chen, W. M., & Wang, X. J. (2023). Cooperation dynamics in collective risk games with endogenous endowments. *Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in a two-stage PGG-CRG model with endogenous endowments, higher perceived risk and lower required contribution ratios increase the proportion of the population that successfully avoids catastrophic loss (target achieved rate). The effect of the first-stage enhancement factor (analogous to MPCR) is non-monotonic: when the required contribution ratio is small, higher enhancement factors help, but at moderate contribution ratios, a moderate enhancement factor is optimal. The model does not include or analyze punishment or sanction mechanisms, so it does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency or related outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or collective risk games, as no punishment or sanction mechanism is present in the model. However, it does provide informative context on how other game design parameters (risk, contribution ratio, enhancement factor) affect cooperation and group success in a threshold public goods/collective risk environment. For the downstream prediction task, this paper should not be used as evidence for the effect of punishment, but may be useful for understanding baseline efficiency or cooperation rates in the absence of punishment.

- source: Weng, Q. F., He, N. R., Hu, L. W., & Chen, X. J. (2021). Heterogeneous investment with dynamical feedback promotes public cooperation and group success in spatial public goods games. *Physics Letters A*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that communication mechanisms—especially chain (one-on-one) communication and the combination of information display with chain communication—substantially increase the frequency and stability of successful group provision of the public good (EPGs via GECs) compared to a baseline with no intervention. Star network (information display only) and global communication also improve outcomes relative to baseline, but less so than chain communication. The most effective intervention is the combination of information display and chain communication. However, the study does not include any punishment or sanctioning treatments, and does not report group efficiency or total payoffs. The findings are thus about behavioral contributions and threshold success, not efficiency per se.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or variants. It is useful for understanding the effects of communication and information display on contribution behavior and threshold success in a TPGG, but cannot inform predictions about punishment-enabled efficiency. None of the experimental treatments include punishment, sanctions, or peer enforcement. Therefore, this paper should not be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency, but may be informative for the effects of communication and information structures in threshold public goods settings.

- source: Ezoe, H., & Ikegawa, Y. (2013). Coexistence of mutualists and non-mutualists in a dual-lattice model. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that spatial structure allows for the persistence and coexistence of mutualists and non-mutualists in a dual-lattice model, even when mutualists pay a cost to benefit others. The parameter range for mutualist persistence expands as the magnitude of interspecific interaction increases. Asymmetry in basic reproduction rates between species can facilitate coexistence. The model does not include punishment or sanction mechanisms, and outcomes are not reported in terms of efficiency or group payoff, but rather in terms of population densities and coexistence patterns.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as it does not include punishment or sanction mechanisms. It is relevant for understanding the effects of spatial structure and interspecific interaction on the persistence of cooperation, but does not inform predictions about efficiency changes due to punishment. The model and findings are adjacent to the prediction task, but not directly informative for the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Liu, L. J., & Chen, X. J. (2018). Evolution of Public Cooperation in a Risky Society with Heterogeneous Assets. *Frontiers in Physics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that asset heterogeneity (a large gap between rich and poor) hinders cooperation, regardless of the temptation to defect. Increasing the value of individual assets or the risk of collective loss promotes cooperation. High thresholds and low risk can destroy cooperation, especially when asset inequality is high. The proportion of poor in the population also affects cooperation: when the poor are a small minority, they tend to free ride; when they are a majority, more contribute. The model does not include punishment or analyze efficiency or group payoff directly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or analyze efficiency or group payoff. However, it is informative about how asset heterogeneity, risk, and threshold affect cooperation rates in threshold public goods games with collective risk. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is not directly relevant, but it may provide contextual insight into how structural features (like risk and heterogeneity) affect cooperation in the absence of punishment.

- source: Shi, Y. D., Zhong, L. X., & Xu, W. J. (2013). Effects of group sensitivity on cooperation in N-person snowdrift game with dynamic grouping. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that in an N-person snowdrift game where agents can adjust their preferred group size based on group sensitivity (a parameter reflecting emotional response to group outcomes), moderate group sensitivity leads to the highest levels of cooperation. Both the cost-to-benefit ratio and group sensitivity affect the evolution of preferred group size and cooperation frequency. There is a functional relationship between the frequency of cooperators and the average preferred group size. However, the paper does not include punishment, sanctions, or direct efficiency/payoff analysis.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games. It is relevant for understanding how group size dynamics and emotional factors (group sensitivity) can affect cooperation rates, but it does not address punishment, sanctions, or efficiency outcomes. Therefore, it should not be used as primary evidence for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects on efficiency.

- source: Liu, Y. Y., Liu, L. J., Guo, R. Q., & Zhang, L. (2023). N-player repeated evolutionary trust game under government management. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a government-managed punishment and reward mechanism in a repeated N-player trust game can sustain trust and investment, leading to higher group payoffs. There is a critical threshold of punishment intensity: below it, untrustworthy behavior dominates and payoffs are low; above it, trust and investment are maintained, and payoffs increase. The model also shows that as punishment intensity increases further, the system can reach a stable coexistence of strategies with high trust and investment. The results are robust across different group sizes and parameter values, and are supported by both theoretical analysis and numerical simulations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in repeated trust-game-like environments with group structure, introducing or increasing the intensity of punishment (and associated rewards for reporting) can substantially increase group efficiency/payoff, provided the punishment is above a critical threshold. The model is not a standard PGG but is closely related, so the qualitative prediction that punishment increases efficiency is likely to transfer to PGG-like settings, especially those with repeated interactions and third-party enforcement. The results are most informative for settings where punishment is implemented by an external authority and is contingent on reporting.

- source: Abbink, K., Sadrieh, A., & Zamir, S. (2004). Fairness, public good, and emotional aspects of punishment behavior. *Theory and Decision*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that making punishment (rejection) visible to proposers increases the frequency of punishment and changes proposer behavior (more equal offers over time), but this does not translate into higher efficiency. Instead, efficiency is lower in the open (punishment-enabled) treatment compared to the covered (punishment-disabled) treatment, because the cost of punishment (lost payoffs from rejections) outweighs any gains from increased fairness. Only when rejection rates are either very high (deterring almost all unfair offers) or very low (almost no punishment) does efficiency approach the maximum. The study provides direct evidence that punishment can reduce efficiency in bargaining environments, especially when it is used at intermediate rates.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling punishment (making it visible and actionable) in a repeated ultimatum minigame reduces efficiency compared to a control where punishment is hidden/disabled. The effect is driven by increased costly punishment (rejections), which reduces total group payoffs. The design is not a standard public goods game, but the punishment mechanism and efficiency analysis are directly relevant. For prediction tasks, this suggests that, in similar two-player bargaining games, enabling punishment is likely to reduce efficiency unless punishment is either very rare or so frequent that it almost eliminates unfair offers. The findings caution against assuming that punishment will improve efficiency in environments where its cost is high and its deterrence effect is partial.

- source: Quan, J., Yang, X. K., Wang, X. J., Yang, J. B., Wu, K. B., & Dai, Z. L. (2019). Withhold-judgment and punishment promote cooperation in indirect reciprocity under incomplete information. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
