The findings are robust across a range of parameterizations, but are limited to behavioral outcomes (cooperation rates) rather than direct payoff or efficiency measures.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in environments structurally similar to a repeated Prisoner's Dilemma with group structure, agent-level punishment (in-group policing) is much more effective at sustaining cooperation than group-level punishment (collective sanctions). However, because the model does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and is not a public goods game, its direct predictive value for efficiency outcomes in PGG-like environments is limited. The results are most useful for understanding the behavioral mechanisms by which punishment can promote or hinder cooperation, and for anticipating that group-level punishment may not translate into higher efficiency or payoff. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that the presence of in-group policing (individualized punishment) is likely to have a more positive effect on cooperation than collective sanctions, but does not provide quantitative efficiency data.

- source: Jaffe, K. (2008). Evolution of Shame as an Adaptation to Social Punishment and its Contribution to Social Cohesiveness. *Complexity*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that shame can evolve as an adaptation to social punishment, increasing pro-social behavior (generosity) after punishment. When punishment is present (especially if costless to the punisher) or when shame is present, populations maintain higher levels of generosity and a balance between punishers and non-punishers. However, the model does not directly report on group efficiency or total payoff, focusing instead on the evolutionary dynamics of behavioral traits. The results suggest that shame and punishment can stabilize cooperation for long periods, but with fluctuations between cooperative and selfish phases.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that punishment (and shame) can increase pro-social behavior in resource-sharing environments, which may translate to higher efficiency in public goods games. However, because the model is not a direct PGG and does not report efficiency or group payoff, its value for predicting efficiency outcomes is limited. The main relevance is in showing that punishment and shame can stabilize cooperation, especially when punishment is not costly to the punisher. The findings are more about evolutionary stability and trait frequencies than about efficiency per se.

- source: Röhl, T., Röhl, C., Schuster, H. G., & Traulsen, A. (2007). Impact of fraud on the mean-field dynamics of cooperative social systems. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in a reputation-based cooperation system (image scoring), the introduction of fraud (scammers who fake cooperation) and punishment (penalties for detected fraud) leads to complex dynamics. If the probability of detecting fraud and the penalty for fraud are high enough, cooperation (discriminators) can dominate. For intermediate detection probabilities, a limited presence of scammers can actually increase the basin of attraction for cooperation, making it easier for cooperation to evolve. The number of rounds of interaction is a key moderator: with more rounds, the payoff advantage of cooperation increases. The model provides explicit conditions (in terms of cost, benefit, penalty, detection probability, and rounds) for when each strategy is favored. The overall effect of punishment on efficiency is mixed: it can increase efficiency by suppressing fraud, but the presence of some fraud can also facilitate cooperation under certain conditions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in reputation-based cooperation games depends on the detection probability, penalty size, cost-benefit ratio, and number of rounds. In games with more rounds and higher detection/penalty, punishment is more likely to increase efficiency by supporting cooperation. However, a limited amount of undetected fraud can sometimes increase the basin of attraction for cooperation, so the relationship is not strictly monotonic. The model is not a standard public goods game, but its structure and payoff logic are closely related. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that the efficiency impact of punishment will be more positive when detection is high, penalties are large, and the game is repeated over more rounds. The presence of fraud and its detectability are important moderators.

- source: Nakamura, M., & Ohtsuki, H. (2014). Indirect reciprocity in three types of social dilemmas. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_tech, reward_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in two-player simultaneous-move social dilemmas, the stability and efficiency of cooperation via indirect reciprocity depend on the structure of the social norm (reputation updating rule) and the underlying game. In Snowdrift and Prisoner's Dilemma, strong punishment of defectors (assigning bad reputations for defection against good players) is necessary to maintain high efficiency (i.e., stable cooperation with high group payoff). In Stag Hunt, such punishment is less critical, and norms that unfairly favor reciprocators (always assign good reputation to reciprocation) can enhance the robustness and efficiency of cooperation. The paper also shows that punishing unconditional cooperators (who help bad players) is important for maintaining efficient cooperation in all three games. The efficiency outcome (i.e., group payoff relative to full cooperation) is maximized under certain social norms, but the effect of punishment-like mechanisms is context-dependent.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment (via reputation loss) on efficiency in public-goods-like games depends on the underlying game structure and the details of the punishment mechanism (social norm). In games with strong temptation to defect (SG, PD), enabling punishment (bad reputation for defectors) increases efficiency by stabilizing cooperation. In coordination games (SH), such punishment is less necessary, and norms that favor reciprocators can be more effective. The findings suggest that, for prediction tasks, the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency should be moderated by the type of social dilemma and the specific implementation of the punishment/reputation mechanism. The results are most directly relevant to two-player games, not standard multi-player PGGs, but the logic may transfer to small-group settings with reputation-based punishment.

- source: Nuño, J. C., Herrero, M. A., & Primicerio, M. (2010). Fighting cheaters: How and how much to invest. *European Journal of Applied Mathematics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The model demonstrates that in the absence of anti-cheating expenditure, cheaters dominate and total wealth collapses. Introducing and increasing expenditure to fight cheating (via social or punitive means) reduces the cheater population and increases total wealth, but only up to an optimal point. Beyond this, further expenditure reduces wealth due to the cost of enforcement. The optimal strategy involves a balance between social promotion and deterrence, with the best compromise (for the parameter values used) being to spend about 5% of wealth on anti-cheating measures, allocating about 90% of that to social promotion. The model also shows that the effectiveness of punishment (police efficiency) is a critical moderator: if punishment is highly effective, more resources should be allocated to it; if not, social measures are preferable. The findings are robust across a range of parameter values and are supported by sensitivity analyses.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that introducing punishment or anti-cheating measures in a public-goods-like environment increases efficiency (total wealth) up to an optimal point, after which further increases in punishment expenditure can reduce efficiency. The model highlights the importance of both the amount spent and the effectiveness of punishment. For prediction tasks, the results suggest that enabling punishment (or anti-cheating expenditure) will increase efficiency relative to the no-punishment baseline, but the magnitude depends on the cost, effectiveness, and allocation of resources. The model also warns that excessive punishment spending can be counterproductive. While the model is not an experimental PGG, its structure and outcomes are closely analogous and informative for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like games.

- source: Fabbri, M., Barbieri, P. N., & Bigoni, M. (2019). Ride Your Luck! A Field Experiment on Lottery-Based Incentives for Compliance. *Management Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: A field experiment on city buses in Italy tested a lottery-based reward for buying tickets, layered on top of existing probabilistic sanctions for fare-dodging. Treated buses saw a significant increase in on-board ticket sales (18–30% increase per hour), with no overall reduction in off-board ticket sales, indicating a net increase in compliance and revenue. The increased revenue covered the cost of the prizes actually claimed, but not the total value of all prizes offered, so cost-effectiveness depended on unclaimed prizes. The effect was not persistent after the lottery ended. Suburban routes showed a substitution from off-board to on-board ticket purchases. The study provides evidence that combining probabilistic sanctions with lottery-based rewards can increase compliance and revenue in a real-world public good context, but the net efficiency gain is limited by the cost of rewards and the sustainability of the effect.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides field evidence that adding a lottery-based reward to an environment with probabilistic sanctions increases compliance and revenue (a proxy for efficiency), but the net efficiency gain depends on the cost of the reward and whether all prizes are claimed. The effect is not persistent after removal. For prediction, the presence of both probabilistic sanctions and a reward mechanism (lottery) increases efficiency relative to sanctions alone, but the magnitude of the gain may be modest and sensitive to the cost structure and salience of the reward. The design is not a standard PGG, but the compliance/public good context and outcome measures are closely related. The findings are most informative for settings where compliance is individually observable and can be incentivized with both sanctions and probabilistic rewards.

- source: Pan, Q. H., Liu, X. S., Bao, H. L., Su, Y., & He, M. F. (2018). Evolution of cooperation through adaptive interaction in a spatial prisoner's dilemma game. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that introducing and increasing the probability of disconnection (β) after defection leads to a significant increase in the fraction of cooperators in both simulation and human lab experiments. The effect is nonlinear: at low β, the effect is modest, but at higher β, cooperation increases sharply. The mechanism is interpreted as a punishment for defection, as defectors are excluded from interactions and thus lose opportunities for payoff. The system can transition between states of all-defector, all-cooperator, or coexistence depending on the parameters (temptation to defect, cost, and β). While payoffs are discussed and collected, the main reported outcome is the fraction of cooperators, not efficiency or total group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that a punishment-like mechanism (disconnection after defection) can substantially increase cooperation rates in a spatial prisoner's dilemma environment. However, it does not directly report efficiency or group payoff as a primary outcome, so its value for predicting efficiency is indirect. The findings suggest that enabling a punishment mechanism that reduces defectors' ability to interact (and thus earn payoff) can increase cooperation, which is likely to improve efficiency in similar environments, especially when the punishment is strong (high β). The design is adjacent to public goods games, so transfer to PGG-like settings should be cautious. The main predictive signal is that punishment mechanisms that exclude defectors can be effective, especially when the exclusion probability is high.

- source: Pan, Q. H., Wang, Y., Chen, Q., Gao, L. Y., & He, M. F. (2022). Effects of quasi-defection strategy on cooperation evolution in social dilemma. *Physics Letters A*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the introduction of quasi-defectors—who can both reward cooperators and punish defectors—enables the stable coexistence of cooperation and quasi-defection, and can suppress defection under certain parameter regimes. The effect of punishment on cooperation is positive but limited: increasing punishment initially increases cooperation, but further increases have diminishing returns. Reward can compensate for the limited effect of punishment and further promote cooperation. The cost of rewarding/punishing must be low for quasi-defectors to persist and support cooperation. The model is theoretical and does not report efficiency or group payoff, but the results suggest that mechanisms combining reward and punishment can sustain higher levels of cooperation than punishment alone.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect theoretical support for the idea that combining punishment and reward mechanisms can promote cooperation in social dilemmas, and that the effect of punishment on cooperation is positive but limited by cost and diminishing returns. However, because the model is based on a prisoner's dilemma with a quasi-defection strategy and does not report efficiency or group payoff, its direct applicability to predicting efficiency in public goods games is limited. The findings suggest that, in analogous settings, moderate punishment and reward can increase cooperation, but the effect on efficiency would depend on the cost structure and the mapping from strategy proportions to group payoff.

- source: Gao, L. Y., Pan, Q. H., & He, M. F. (2022). Advanced defensive cooperators promote cooperation in the prisoner's dilemma game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing advanced defensive cooperators, who can identify and punish anti-defensive defectors at a cost, leads to higher equilibrium fractions of cooperators compared to the standard prisoner's dilemma. The presence of advanced defensive cooperators is especially important when the temptation to defect is high. The model shows that the effectiveness of advanced defensive cooperation depends on parameters such as the temptation value, defensive benefit, defensive loss, fine (punishment), and screening cost. While the model tracks payoffs in the replicator equations, the main reported results are the fractions of each strategy at equilibrium, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that punishment-like mechanisms (here, advanced defensive cooperation with screening and fines) can increase the prevalence of cooperation in social dilemma settings. However, because the main outcomes are strategy fractions rather than group efficiency or total payoff, the results are only adjacent to the prediction task of forecasting efficiency. The model is a prisoner's dilemma variant, not a public goods game, and does not report efficiency as a ratio of actual to maximum possible group payoff. Thus, while the findings suggest that enabling punishment can increase cooperation, they do not provide direct quantitative evidence for predicting efficiency changes in public-goods-game-like environments.

- source: Przepiorka, W., & Diekmann, A. (2020). Binding Contracts, Non-Binding Promises and Social Feedback in the Intertemporal Common-Pool Resource Game. *Games*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: In a repeated intertemporal common-pool resource game with five players and ten rounds, the study compares the effects of (1) binding contracts, (2) non-binding promises, (3) private social feedback, and (4) public social feedback, against a no-regulation control. Binding contracts, when unanimously signed, lead to the highest group efficiency (average profit per round close to the cooperative optimum). Non-binding promises increase sustainable investment and efficiency initially, but the effect decays over time. Private feedback (where only the recipient sees ratings) does not improve efficiency over the control. Public feedback (where feedback scores are visible to all) significantly increases sustainable investment and group efficiency, with the effect being more stable over time than promises. The study provides direct evidence that public, but not private, social feedback can substitute for formal sanctions in promoting efficiency in CPR dilemmas.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in repeated CPR games with continuous contribution choices, public social feedback (where feedback is visible to all) can substantially increase group efficiency, even in the absence of formal punishment or monetary sanctions. Private feedback is ineffective. Binding contracts are most effective but require unanimous agreement. For prediction tasks, the presence of public feedback (show_other_summaries = yes, show_punishment_id = yes) should be expected to increase efficiency relative to control, while private feedback (show_other_summaries = yes, show_punishment_id = no) should not. The absence of monetary punishment or reward mechanisms means that the findings are most relevant for settings where social (non-monetary) sanctions are possible. The game design dimensions (player_count = 5, num_rounds = 10, continuous contributions, no chat, known rounds, full feedback on others' choices and payoffs) are well-specified and should be used as reference points for prediction.

- source: Ohdaira, T. (2021). Cooperation evolves by the payoff-difference-based probabilistic reward. *European Physical Journal B*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The study finds that introducing a probabilistic reward mechanism, where the probability of rewarding a cooperator increases with the payoff difference between the player and the cooperator, leads to higher average payoffs and more cooperation in most cases. The effect is robust across regular, random, and scale-free network topologies, though the strength and range of the effect depend on the network structure and the reward parameter t. The paper does not directly study punishment in this model, but situates its findings in the context of prior work on punishment and reward in public goods and prisoner's dilemma games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that a well-designed reward mechanism can increase group efficiency (average payoff) in spatial prisoner's dilemma games, especially under certain network structures and parameter ranges. However, it does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency, nor does it manipulate or compare punishment and reward within the same experimental or simulation framework. For prediction tasks focused on punishment effects in public-goods-like environments, this paper is only indirectly informative, mainly by analogy and through its discussion of prior punishment studies.

- source: Fishman, M. A. (2006). Involuntary defection and the evolutionary origins of empathy. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated dyadic cooperation games with involuntary defection, unconditional punishment (retaliation for any defection) undermines cooperation and reduces efficiency, as it leads to cycles of mutual defection. In contrast, an empathic retaliator strategy—where punishment is withheld if the opponent's defection follows one's own involuntary defection—can be evolutionarily stable and support high efficiency, especially when the expected number of repeated interactions is large. The stability and efficiency of cooperation depend on the cost of helping, the probability of being able to help, and the expected number of interaction cycles. The paper provides explicit conditions and payoff matrices for these outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that the design of punishment mechanisms critically affects efficiency in repeated cooperation games. Specifically, unconditional punishment (retaliation) in the presence of involuntary defection leads to lower efficiency, while empathic (conditional) punishment can sustain high efficiency if repeated interactions are frequent. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency depends on whether punishment is unconditional or conditional/empathic, the frequency of repeated interactions, and the possibility of involuntary defection. However, the model is not a public goods game, and punishment is not costly in the sense of standard PGG experiments; rather, it is refusal to help. Thus, direct quantitative predictions for PGGs should be made with caution, but the qualitative insight is that punishment can either harm or help efficiency depending on its design and the game context.

- source: Gutnisky, D. A., & Zanutto, B. S. (2004). Cooperation in the iterated prisoner's dilemma is learned by operant conditioning mechanisms. *Artificial Life*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, reward_exists, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The operant conditioning model (ZL) can learn to cooperate in the IPD and achieves high average payoffs against a variety of strategies. In evolutionary simulations, ZL can dominate or survive in populations depending on the mix of strategies and the presence of noise (random moves). However, ZL is always slightly less efficient than non-learning strategies because it requires time to learn optimal responses. The model does not include explicit punishment or sanctioning mechanisms; instead, it relies on reinforcement learning from payoffs. The findings are relevant for understanding how learning mechanisms can support cooperation and efficiency in repeated social dilemmas, but do not directly address the effect of enabling or disabling punishment in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does, however, provide insight into how learning mechanisms (operant conditioning) can support cooperation and high payoffs in repeated dyadic dilemmas (IPD). The absence of explicit punishment or sanctioning means that the findings are only adjacent to the prediction task. The results suggest that learning-based strategies can achieve high efficiency, but may be less efficient than fixed strategies in the short run due to learning costs. For prediction tasks focused on punishment-enabled vs. punishment-disabled efficiency, this paper offers only indirect, contextual support.

- source: Ohdaira, T. (2024). The universal probabilistic reward based on the difference of payoff realizes the evolution of cooperation. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a universal probabilistic reward mechanism, where players with higher payoffs reward their neighbors (cooperators or defectors) with a probability proportional to the payoff difference, leads to a significant increase in both cooperation and average group payoff (efficiency) in spatial prisoner's dilemma games. The effect is robust across different network topologies (regular, random, scale-free) and a range of reward coefficients (t), except when t=0 or t=1, where the mechanism is inactive. The study provides detailed analysis of how the speed and stability of cooperation and efficiency depend on the reward parameter and network structure. No punishment mechanism is implemented in the model, but the paper situates its contribution in the context of prior work on both reward and punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that a universal, payoff-difference-based reward mechanism can substantially increase group efficiency in spatial prisoner's dilemma games, even when rewards are given to both cooperators and defectors. While the study does not manipulate or analyze punishment, it is highly informative for predicting the effects of reward-based interventions on efficiency, especially in networked or spatially structured games. The findings suggest that, in the absence of punishment, well-designed reward mechanisms can achieve high efficiency. However, the results may not directly transfer to public goods games with explicit punishment options, as the model does not include a punishment treatment or control. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the idea that reward mechanisms (especially those not limited to cooperators) can be highly effective at increasing efficiency, but does not provide direct evidence about the marginal effect of adding punishment to a baseline.

- source: Murase, Y., & Baek, S. K. (2023). Grouping promotes both partnership and rivalry with long memory in direct reciprocity. *Plos Computational Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The main finding is that group structure, when combined with the availability of longer-memory strategies (which allow for friendly rival strategies), leads to the evolution of nearly full cooperation and maximal efficiency in the repeated donation game. In well-mixed populations, longer memory does not have this effect. The results are robust to the benefit-to-cost ratio and to details of the evolutionary process. The paper does not study public goods games or explicit punishment mechanisms, but the repeated donation game is adjacent to PGGs, and friendly rival strategies function as a form of endogenous sanctioning (never allowing the co-player to earn more). Efficiency is measured as the fraction of time mutual cooperation is achieved, which is equivalent to group payoff relative to the maximum.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in two-player repeated donation games (adjacent to PGGs), the combination of group structure and the availability of longer-memory strategies (enabling friendly rival strategies) can dramatically increase efficiency, even when cooperation would otherwise be difficult. While there is no explicit punishment mechanism, friendly rival strategies act as endogenous enforcers of cooperation by never allowing the co-player to earn more. For prediction tasks, this suggests that in environments with group structure and sufficient strategic complexity, efficiency can be much higher than in well-mixed or memory-limited settings, even without explicit punishment. However, the results may not transfer directly to multi-player PGGs or to settings with explicit costly punishment.

- source: Trejo, K. K., Juarez, R., Clempner, J. B., & Poznyak, A. S. (2023). Non-Cooperative Bargaining with Unsophisticated Agents. *Computational Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing penalty mechanisms for deviation and time in bargaining games with myopic agents ensures convergence to efficient (Pareto optimal or Nash) outcomes, but at a cost: the process is slower and less efficient than with fully rational, forward-looking agents. The efficiency loss is quantifiable as the 'price of unsophistication,' and depends on the penalty parameters and the agents' behavior. The results are robust across a wide class of utility functions and game structures, including Markov processes. The paper does not study public goods games or peer punishment, but the penalty mechanism is analogous to centralized sanctions rather than decentralized peer punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is not directly informative for predicting the effect of peer punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it studies bargaining games with centralized penalty mechanisms and myopic agents. However, it provides indirect evidence that introducing penalty costs can improve convergence to efficient outcomes even with unsophisticated agents, though always with some efficiency loss relative to the ideal. The results suggest that the design and magnitude of penalties (punishment costs) are critical for achieving near-efficient outcomes, but the context is bargaining, not public goods provision. The findings may be useful for understanding the general role of sanctions in promoting efficiency, but should be transferred to PGG-like settings with caution.

- source: Iwamura, Y., Nagashima, K., & Tanimoto, J. (2020). Evolutionary dynamics of a 3-strategy game: Cooperator, defector and costly cooperative loner strategic types. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The introduction of a costly cooperative loner (CL) as a third strategy in a prisoner's dilemma game does not prevent the collapse to all-defection, nor does it improve social average payoff (efficiency) compared to the standard two-strategy game. The CL strategy is always outcompeted and goes extinct, after which defectors dominate. This is in contrast to models with costly punishment, where punishment can sometimes sustain cooperation and higher efficiency. The paper provides a detailed analysis of the payoff matrix and evolutionary trajectories, showing that the addition of CL does not alter the efficiency outcome: the system always ends in the least efficient (all-defector) state.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides a negative case: simply adding a third strategy that is not an active punisher (but pays a cost to avoid defectors) does not improve efficiency in a prisoner's dilemma environment. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of a 'costly loner' or similar non-punishing third strategy is not sufficient to increase efficiency. The results reinforce that only certain types of third strategies (e.g., active costly punishment or rewarding) can shift efficiency outcomes. The findings are most relevant for infinite, well-mixed populations and may not generalize to finite or structured populations. The paper does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment per se, but its comparison to punishment models supports the view that punishment (with sufficient impact and cost structure) is necessary for efficiency gains in these settings.

- source: Forges, F., Horst, U., & Salomon, A. (2016). Feasibility and individual rationality in two-person Bayesian games. *International Journal of Game Theory*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in two-person Bayesian games with a single informed player, the existence of a uniform punishment strategy (UPS) for the uninformed player guarantees the existence of feasible, individually rational (i.e., efficient/welfare-maximizing) solutions. These solutions can be implemented as Nash equilibria of appropriately designed noncooperative games. The set of such payoffs coincides with the set of joint plan equilibrium payoffs in the infinitely repeated game. When UPS does not hold, feasible, individually rational solutions may not exist. The results generalize to cases where both players have private information, under certain conditions. The paper provides theoretical foundations for how punishment strategies can support efficient outcomes in games with incomplete information, but does not provide empirical estimates or experimental results.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that the ability to punish (specifically, the existence of a uniform punishment strategy) is crucial for achieving efficient, individually rational outcomes in two-person Bayesian games with incomplete information. For the downstream prediction task, the main implication is that the presence and effectiveness of punishment mechanisms (punishment cost, punishment tech, and the ability to credibly threaten punishment) are key moderators of whether efficiency gains from punishment are possible. However, the paper does not provide quantitative predictions or empirical estimates, and its results are not specific to public goods games or experimental designs. It is most useful for understanding the theoretical boundary conditions under which punishment can support efficiency, rather than for direct prediction of efficiency changes in experimental PGG-like environments.

- source: Ille, S. (2021). The evolution of sectarianism. *Communications in Nonlinear Science and Numerical Simulation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that the evolution of coercive (punishment-like) strategies in a repeated bargaining game can lead to either efficient egalitarian outcomes or inefficient sectarian contracts, depending on the relative costs and benefits of coercion and the structure of social learning. When the cost of coercion is low and potential gains from inegalitarian contracts are high, societies are more likely to settle into inefficient, conflictual, or sectarian equilibria. When both groups have similar coercive capacities and the cost of coercion is high, egalitarian and efficient outcomes are more likely. The model also shows that collective memory, identity politics, and initial asymmetries can tip the balance toward persistent conflict or sectarianism, reducing overall efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and simulation-based evidence that the effect of punishment-like mechanisms (coercion) on efficiency is highly context-dependent. If punishment is symmetric and costly, it can support efficient cooperation (egalitarian contracts). If punishment is asymmetric or the gains from inegalitarian outcomes are high, punishment can entrench inefficient, conflictual, or exploitative equilibria. The model suggests that game design dimensions such as the cost and effectiveness of punishment, the symmetry of player power, and the structure of social learning critically moderate the efficiency impact of punishment. However, the model is not a direct PGG or public goods game, but rather a repeated bargaining/coordination game with endogenous punishment-like coercion. Thus, its findings are most relevant for adjacent settings where punishment can be used to enforce either efficient or inefficient group outcomes.

- source: Huang, S. X., Liu, X. S., Hu, Y. H., & Fu, X. (2023). The influence of aggressive behavior on cooperation evolution in social dilemma. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing aggressive behavior (a form of costly punishment) into a prisoner's dilemma game can promote the persistence of cooperation by deterring defectors. Aggressive cooperators are able to suppress defectors, especially when the benefit of aggression (compensation) is high and the risk (mutual loss) is not too great. The system can reach various equilibria, including coexistence of multiple strategies, depending on parameter values. When the temptation to defect is low and aggressive compensation is high, cooperation dominates. As the temptation to defect increases, defectors re-emerge, but aggressive defectors can also help limit the dominance of non-aggressive defectors, allowing some cooperation to survive. The findings suggest that punishment-like mechanisms can improve group outcomes compared to a baseline with only cooperation and defection, though the paper does not quantify efficiency in the standard sense.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence that introducing a punishment-like mechanism (aggressive behavior) in a prisoner's dilemma environment can increase group payoff and support cooperation, especially when the cost-benefit tradeoff of punishment is favorable and the temptation to defect is not too high. For prediction tasks, the results suggest that enabling punishment (with moderate cost and sufficient effectiveness) is likely to improve efficiency relative to a no-punishment baseline, particularly in environments where the risk of mutual punishment is not prohibitive. However, the lack of explicit efficiency ratios and the focus on evolutionary stability rather than direct payoff comparisons limit the precision of quantitative predictions.

- source: Li, S. J., Bao, W., Dai, Y. Y., Ye, Y., & Xie, N. G. (2022). Emotional game and the evolution of cooperation considering the effects of reputation. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that adding a reputation mechanism based on emotional profiles to a voluntary prisoner's dilemma game (VPDG) substantially increases both the frequency of cooperation and the average payoff of the population. The reputation mechanism works by making individuals more likely to cooperate with those who have good reputations and less likely to cooperate with those who have bad reputations, effectively integrating both reward and punishment effects. Compared to previous models (VPDG alone and VPDG with emotion but no reputation), the model with reputation achieves higher average payoffs and more stable cooperation, especially in regions of the parameter space where cooperation would otherwise be low. The effect is strongest when the temptation to defect is not too high (T < 1.5), but even at higher temptations, the reputation mechanism maintains higher cooperation and payoff than the baselines.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong simulation-based evidence that mechanisms integrating both reward and punishment via reputation can substantially increase group efficiency in voluntary public-goods-like games. While the model is not a standard PGG, the adjacent structure (VPDG with loner option) and the explicit reporting of average payoff as a welfare/efficiency measure make the findings relevant for predicting the effect of adding punishment/reward/reputation mechanisms in similar environments. The results suggest that, when the control (no reputation) game has moderate or low efficiency, enabling a reputation mechanism (which includes both positive and negative sanctions) can drive efficiency close to the cooperative maximum, especially when the temptation to defect is not extreme. However, the findings are based on simulation, not human experiments, and the specific implementation of reputation is more complex than simple punishment. The model does not directly manipulate all design dimensions (e.g., player count, chat, etc.), so predictions should be adjusted accordingly.

- source: Wang, W. J., Xu, Z. H., Hua, S. J., Cui, L. Q., Zhang, J. L., & Meng, F. Y. (2024). Threshold-initiated spatial public goods games. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that intergroup competition significantly increases the rate of volunteering in the volunteer's dilemma, even when there is no additional monetary incentive or change in the payoff structure. Risk (a 50% chance of public good failure) reduces volunteering rates, but intergroup competition mitigates this negative effect. Gender and risk aversion moderate these effects: females volunteer more in no-risk settings, males increase volunteering more under risky intergroup competition. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, but notes in the discussion that over-volunteering (stimulated by intergroup competition) is socially inefficient.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or group payoff in public goods games with or without punishment. Its findings are about volunteering rates in a volunteer's dilemma, which is structurally adjacent to but not the same as a standard public goods game. The results suggest that intergroup competition increases pro-social action rates, especially under risk, but also that this can lead to inefficiency through over-volunteering. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, the main takeaway is that mechanisms increasing pro-social action (like competition) may not always improve efficiency, especially in anti-coordination settings like the volunteer's dilemma. However, the lack of direct efficiency or payoff data limits the paper's predictive value for the target task.

- source: Bruner, J. P., & Smead, R. (2022). Tag-based spite with correlated interactions. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that the introduction of observable tags and the possibility of conditioning behavior on tags (e.g., only harming out-group members) fundamentally changes the evolutionary dynamics of costly harming (spite) in the Prisoner's Delight game. Tags make pure (unconditional) spite less likely to evolve even when anti-correlation would otherwise favor it, but make conditional (tag-based) spite more likely in some regimes. Several stable equilibria are identified, including cooperative polymorphisms (mostly cooperation, little spite), pure spite, and a novel 'treacherous polymorphism' where spite is directed within groups. The presence of tags generally reduces the prevalence of widespread spiteful behavior, even under conditions that would otherwise favor it. The results are framed in terms of evolutionary stability and behavioral frequencies, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants. The model is a two-player game (Prisoner's Delight) with costly harming, not a multi-player public goods game. The main outcomes are the evolutionary frequencies of behavioral types, not group efficiency or total payoff. However, the analysis is relevant for understanding how the structure of punishment-like mechanisms (costly harming, conditional on observable tags) and population structure (correlated/anti-correlated interactions) can affect the prevalence of cooperation and spite. The findings suggest that mechanisms allowing for conditional punishment (e.g., based on group identity) can both promote and suppress harmful behavior, depending on the context. For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides indirect theoretical context about how punishment-like behaviors may interact with population structure and signaling, but does not provide quantitative or empirical evidence for efficiency outcomes in PGG-like environments.

- source: Hu, Q., Jin, T., Jiang, Y. L., & Liu, X. W. (2024). Reputation incentives with public supervision promote cooperation in evolutionary games. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
