  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, reward_exists, reward_tech
  findings: The study finds that a reputation incentive mechanism with public supervision can significantly increase the fraction of cooperators in a spatial PDG. The effect is stronger when the evaluation intensity (the range of possible evaluation values) is lower, allowing players to more quickly update their evaluations and reputations. The model demonstrates that reputation-based rewards can compensate cooperators for losses due to defectors, promoting the formation and persistence of cooperative clusters. However, the paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, so its findings are limited to behavioral outcomes (cooperation rates) rather than payoff-based outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that reputation-based reward mechanisms with public supervision can increase cooperation rates in spatial PDG-like environments, especially when evaluation intensity is low. However, because the model is not a public goods game and does not report efficiency or group payoff, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGGs. The findings may be useful for understanding the role of reputation and reward mechanisms as adjacent or complementary to punishment, but should not be used as direct evidence for efficiency outcomes in PGGs with punishment.

- source: Zhang, N., Zhang, X. X., Lei, M., & Yang, Y. J. (2020). Multiagent Collaborative Governance for Targeted Poverty Alleviation from the Perspective of Stakeholders. *Complexity*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that: (1) Increasing support from local governments and social organizations to poverty groups can promote the willingness of poverty groups to cooperate, but excessive support from higher-level government does not necessarily lead to more cooperation or better outcomes. (2) Moderate increases in punishment (negative returns) for noncooperation by social organizations and poverty groups can encourage cooperative behavior. (3) Increasing the basic economic benefits for social organizations and poverty groups significantly increases their willingness to cooperate. The model suggests that a combination of incentives and moderate sanctions is more effective than top-down subsidies alone. However, the main outcomes are the equilibrium rates of cooperation, not efficiency or group payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, contextual evidence for the prediction task. It does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, but it does analyze how punishment (modeled as negative returns for noncooperation) and other design parameters affect the evolution of cooperation in a multi-agent setting. The findings suggest that moderate punishment can increase cooperation rates, which in standard public goods games is often associated with higher efficiency, but this is not directly demonstrated here. The model is more complex than standard PGGs, involving three types of agents and context-specific payoffs. Thus, the paper is best used for qualitative, indirect support: it reinforces that punishment and incentives can promote cooperation, but does not provide quantitative estimates of efficiency effects for PGG-like environments.

- source: Li, G. Y., Du, H. F., & He, X. C. (2024). The evolutionary prisoner's dilemma game in continuous signed networks. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that negative relationships, modeled as punitive ties that reduce payoffs for defectors, can promote the emergence and stability of cooperation in networked prisoner's dilemma games. The effect is stronger when the negative coefficient (punishment strength) or the proportion of negative ties is higher. Adaptive networks, where agents can adjust their ties, further facilitate cooperation. Structural balance (alignment of positive ties within groups and negative ties between groups) also supports higher cooperation. However, the paper does not report on group efficiency, welfare, or total payoff, focusing instead on the behavioral outcome of cooperation rates.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that punishment-like mechanisms (here, negative relationships that reduce payoffs) can increase cooperation rates in networked social dilemmas. However, because the main outcome is the proportion of cooperators rather than efficiency or group payoff, and because the model is a prisoner's dilemma rather than a public goods game, its direct value for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments is limited. The findings suggest that increasing the cost or prevalence of punishment can promote cooperation, but do not quantify the effect on group efficiency or welfare.

- source: Koike, S., Nakamaru, M., & Tsujimoto, M. (2010). Evolution of cooperation in rotating indivisible goods game. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in a rotating indivisible goods game (a model of ROSCAs), cooperation collapses unless both a peer selection rule (based on reputation) and a forfeiture rule (excluding non-contributors from receiving goods) are present. Peer selection alone or forfeiture alone is insufficient. The model tracks the evolution of cooperation probabilities before and after receiving goods, showing that only the combination of both rules leads to sustained high cooperation. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, so its findings are limited to behavioral outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, contextual evidence for the prediction task. While it does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes, it shows that in a public-goods-adjacent environment with indivisible goods, the presence of both peer selection (reputation-based group formation) and a forfeiture rule (exclusion of non-contributors) is necessary to sustain high cooperation rates. For prediction, this suggests that punishment-like mechanisms (here, exclusion and peer selection) may only be effective in combination, and that the structure of the game (indivisible goods, rotation, exclusion) is critical. However, the lack of direct efficiency or payoff data limits its direct applicability to efficiency prediction.

- source: Wu, Y., Zhang, Z. P., Wang, X. Y., & Chang, S. H. (2019). Impact of probabilistic incentives on the evolution of cooperation in complex topologies. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that probabilistic positive incentives (rewards) for cooperators promote the evolution and maintenance of cooperation, while probabilistic negative incentives (anti-social punishment) undermine it. These effects are robust across different network structures (square lattice, Erdös-Rényi, Barabási-Albert) and for both the prisoner's dilemma and snowdrift games. The main dependent variable is the stationary fraction of cooperators. The paper does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, so its findings are limited to behavioral outcomes (cooperation rates) rather than payoff-based outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the likely direction of punishment and reward effects on cooperation rates in two-player evolutionary games, but does not provide direct evidence about efficiency or group payoff. Its results suggest that positive incentives (rewards) increase cooperation and negative incentives (anti-social punishment) decrease it, but the transferability to public goods games and to efficiency outcomes is limited. The absence of payoff or efficiency analysis means this paper should be used only as weak, indirect support for predicting efficiency effects of punishment or reward in public goods game-like environments.

- source: Deng, L. L., Zhang, X. X., & Wang, C. (2021). Coevolution of spatial ultimatum game and link weight promotes fairness. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, reward_exists
  findings: The coevolution of game strategy and link weight in a spatial ultimatum game model leads to higher average proposal and acceptance levels, interpreted as greater fairness. The key driver is the link weight boundary control parameter (\delta), which, when increased, allows for greater heterogeneity in social ties and promotes fairer strategies. The adjustment amplitude (\Delta) has a smaller or context-dependent effect. The model does not analyze or report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, so its direct relevance to efficiency prediction in public goods or similar games is limited.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The model is an ultimatum game (not a PGG or direct variant), and the main outcomes are behavioral (fairness, proposal/acceptance levels) rather than payoff-based. The 'punishment' in this context is the weakening of social ties, not costly punishment of the type used in PGGs. Therefore, while the findings may be conceptually adjacent—suggesting that dynamic social relationships can promote fairer behavior—they do not inform quantitative predictions about efficiency or payoff outcomes in PGGs with or without punishment.

- source: Calderón, J. P., & Zarama, R. (2006). How learning affects the evolution of strong reciprocity. *Adaptive Behavior*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in evolutionary simulations of the ultimatum game, agents with greater phenotypic plasticity (ability to adapt their offer and acceptance strategies during their lifetime) evolve to make higher offers and demand higher minimum acceptable offers, compared to rigid agents. This is interpreted as the emergence of strong reciprocity and altruistic punishment. The diversity of behaviors also increases with plasticity. However, the study does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and all outcomes are at the level of individual behavioral strategies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the evolution of punishment and cooperation in a bargaining game (ultimatum game), not a public goods game. It does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. However, it suggests that mechanisms increasing behavioral diversity and adaptability can promote more cooperative and punishing behaviors. The findings are only adjacent to the prediction task, as the game structure, outcome measures, and focus differ from standard PGGs.

- source: Zhang, Y. L., Yang, S., Chen, X. J., Bai, Y. B., & Xie, G. M. (2023). Reputation update of responders efficiently promotes the evolution of fairness in the ultimatum game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that indirect reciprocity mechanisms based on reputation can strongly promote fairness in the ultimatum game. Specifically, updating responders' reputation via image scoring leads to a very high level of fairness (over 95%), while updating proposers' reputation alone increases fairness above the baseline (no reputation) for many social norms, with a few norms achieving over 80% fairness. When both proposers' and responders' reputations are updated, most social norms yield even higher fairness than updating proposers' reputation alone, but never exceed the effect of updating responders' reputation alone. The results are based on evolutionary game-theoretic modeling and analysis of strategy dynamics, not on empirical or experimental data. The main dependent variable is the frequency of fair offers and rejections of unfair offers, not group payoff or efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, adjacent evidence for the prediction task. While it does not study public goods games or efficiency/payoff outcomes directly, it analyzes how indirect reciprocity and reputation mechanisms affect fairness (a behavioral outcome) in the ultimatum game, which is a related but distinct environment. The findings suggest that mechanisms analogous to punishment (here, rejection of unfair offers, i.e., altruistic punishment) can strongly promote norm compliance and fairness, especially when reputation is at stake. However, because the outcome is not efficiency or group payoff, and the game is not a public goods game, the results should be used only as weak, indirect evidence for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments.

- source: Wang, J., Tian, J. C., & Li, X. P. (2024). Enhancement of cooperation induced by information-payoff evolution on two-layer complex networks. *Physics Letters A*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that introducing dynamic elimination (removal of defectors) in a networked donation game greatly increases cooperation frequency, but at the cost of eliminating many players. Adding the ZD strategy further increases cooperation and allows more players to remain in the game. The combination of elimination and ZD strategy is robust to changes in game parameters and identification errors. Larger-degree nodes are more likely to survive, and the optimal initial strategy for survival depends on whether ZD is present. However, the study does not report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, focusing instead on behavioral and structural outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that mechanisms analogous to punishment (elimination of defectors) and sophisticated strategies (ZD) can increase cooperation and player retention in dynamic networked games. However, because it does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The findings may be useful for understanding the behavioral and structural consequences of punishment-like mechanisms, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes.

- source: Tanimoto, J., & Sagara, H. (2015). How the indirect reciprocity with co-evolving norm and strategy for 2 x 2 prisoner's dilemma game works for emerging cooperation. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The study finds that stable cooperation via indirect reciprocity is more robust in environments with a stronger dilemma (higher temptation to defect), as this encourages the evolution of norms that punish second-order free-riders. In contrast, weaker dilemmas allow permissive norms to dominate, leading to fragile cooperation. Allowing agents to interact with others holding different norms undermines indirect reciprocity unless the image score is evaluated by the focal agent. Introducing a small action error (randomness) can help robust, punishing norms emerge by preventing the early dominance of overly permissive norms. The main dependent variable is the cooperation fraction; average payoff is shown but not analyzed in depth.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the role of norm-based punishment (targeting second-order free-riders) in sustaining cooperation in social dilemma environments. However, it does not use a public goods game, does not implement explicit costly punishment, and does not report efficiency or group payoff as a main outcome. The findings suggest that mechanisms which allow for the punishment of second-order free-riders (whether via norms or explicit punishment) may be important for robust cooperation, especially in more challenging (stronger dilemma) environments. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the idea that the structure of sanctioning (including norm evolution and the ability to punish non-punishers) can be critical, but it does not provide direct quantitative evidence for efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like games.

- source: Li, S. L., He, Z. X., Jia, D. Y., Shen, C., Shi, L., & Tanimoto, J. (2024). Granting leaders priority exit options promotes and jeopardizes cooperation in social dilemmas. *Neurocomputing*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that granting exit rights to high-degree (leader) nodes in a spatial prisoner's dilemma can either suppress or promote cooperation, depending on the strength of the social dilemma and the network's assortativity. In weak dilemmas, leader-priority exit reduces cooperation, while in strong dilemmas, it can promote cooperation by isolating defectors and allowing cooperative clusters to survive among low-degree nodes. Random and fringe-priority exit have weaker or negligible effects. The main outcome is the relative cooperation ratio among non-exiters. The model does not report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, and is not a public goods game. The exit option is not a punishment mechanism per se, but can act as a hard sanction by denying payoffs to others.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is not directly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The game is a spatial prisoner's dilemma with an exit option, not a PGG, and the main outcome is cooperation rate, not efficiency or group payoff. The exit option is not a punishment mechanism, though it can function as a hard sanction. The findings may be relevant for understanding how voluntary exclusion or exit options affect cooperation in networked dilemmas, but do not provide direct evidence for the efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments. Use this paper only for very indirect, mechanistic insights about how exclusion or exit options might interact with network structure and dilemma strength.

- source: Gao, L. Y., Pan, Q. H., & He, M. F. (2021). Environmental-based defensive promotes cooperation in the prisoner's dilemma game. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that allowing cooperators to take defensive actions (at a cost) in response to environmental cues (number of other cooperators) can prevent the collapse to full defection and promote higher levels of cooperation. The effectiveness of this mechanism depends on the defensive cost, defensive benefit, defensive loss, and the threshold for defensive awareness. When defensive measures are sufficiently beneficial and not too costly, and when cooperators are sufficiently vigilant (high threshold and probability), the system can reach full cooperation or stable coexistence. However, the main reported outcome is the fraction of cooperators, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect theoretical evidence that mechanisms allowing individuals to reduce exploitation (analogous to defensive or sanctioning actions) can increase cooperation rates in social dilemmas. However, because the model is based on the prisoner's dilemma (not a public goods game) and reports cooperation fraction rather than efficiency or group payoff, its direct value for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments is limited. The results suggest that the effectiveness of defensive/sanctioning mechanisms depends on their cost, benefit, and the conditions under which they are triggered, which may inform the expected direction of punishment effects in related models. However, quantitative predictions for efficiency cannot be extracted from this paper.

- source: Gao, L. Y., Pan, Q. H., & He, M. F. (2021). Effects of defensive cooperation strategy on the evolution of cooperation in social dilemma. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The introduction of a defensive cooperation strategy (where cooperators pay a cost to reduce losses against defectors) can promote and sustain higher levels of cooperation in both well-mixed and structured populations, compared to the standard prisoner's dilemma. The effectiveness of this strategy depends on the balance between the defensive cost and defensive benefit. When the defensive benefit exceeds the cost, defensive cooperators can suppress defectors and allow cooperation to persist. However, excessive defensive benefit can lead to second-order free-riding by unconditional cooperators. The model shows cyclic dominance among the three strategies in structured populations. The paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, so its findings are not directly translatable to efficiency prediction tasks.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. Its model is a theoretical extension of the prisoner's dilemma with a defensive cooperation strategy, which is conceptually adjacent to punishment but not equivalent. The outcomes are behavioral (strategy fractions), not payoff-based. Therefore, while the paper suggests that self-interested defensive strategies can promote cooperation under certain conditions, it does not provide quantitative or qualitative evidence about efficiency or group payoff that can be directly used for the downstream prediction task. Its relevance is limited to mechanism discussion and indirect contextualization.

- source: Cui, G. H., Wang, Z., Ren, J. K., Lu, K., & Li, M. C. (2016). Promotion of cooperation induced by discriminators in the spatial multi-player donor-recipient game. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The introduction of a discriminative cooperation strategy in a spatial multi-player donor-recipient game can maintain high levels of cooperation and prevent the dominance of defectors, especially when the cost-to-benefit ratio is low and the cost of information collection is small. Discriminators act as conditional cooperators who deny resources to defectors, which functions as a form of indirect punishment. The model shows that, under certain parameter regimes, all three strategies (cooperation, defection, discrimination) can coexist in a cyclic dominance pattern, and the ratio of donated to total resources can remain high. However, the model does not directly report group efficiency or total welfare in the sense of public goods games, and the structure is not a standard PGG.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that conditional, discriminative strategies (akin to targeted punishment or exclusion) can sustain cooperation and high resource donation in spatial multi-player settings, especially when the cost of such discrimination is low. However, because the model is not a standard public goods game and does not report group efficiency or total payoff, its findings should be transferred to PGG-like prediction tasks with caution. The main implication is that mechanisms which allow players to conditionally withhold benefits from defectors (even at a cost) can be effective in maintaining cooperation, but the quantitative effect on efficiency in PGGs is not directly established.

- source: Tao, Y. W., Hu, K. P., & Shi, L. (2021). Risk-preference-driven participate willingness provides alternative routes to solve social dilemma. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a partial cooperation strategy (representing moderate risk preference) into a spatial voluntary prisoner's dilemma leads to rich evolutionary dynamics, including new forms of cyclic dominance and phase transitions between different stable strategy combinations. The willingness to participate parameter (u) determines whether partial cooperators act more like cooperators or loners, affecting which strategies coexist. The presence of partial cooperators can reduce the prevalence of unconditional cooperators and inhibit second-order free-riding. However, the study does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or the effects of punishment or reward mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. The model is adjacent to the PGG family (voluntary PD with loner and partial cooperation strategies) and focuses on risk preference and participation willingness. The findings may be relevant for understanding how participation options and risk attitudes affect cooperation dynamics, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency under punishment. Thus, this paper should not be weighted heavily for the downstream prediction task.

- source: Newton, P. K., & Ma, Y. (2021). Maximizing cooperation in the prisoner's dilemma evolutionary game via optimal control. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The paper shows that by optimally controlling the off-diagonal elements of the prisoner's dilemma payoff matrix (interpreted as dynamically changing incentives and penalties), it is possible to minimize the defector population (maximize cooperation) over a fixed time horizon. The optimal control is bang-bang and depends on the initial state. The reduction in defectors is most efficient for small initial defector populations. The method cycles through a sequence of different games (PD, Leader, Deadlock, etc.) to achieve the optimal outcome. The approach is adaptive over multiple cycles, recalculating the optimal schedule based on the current state. However, the study does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, and is not based on experimental or empirical data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It is a theoretical study of optimal control in a two-population evolutionary prisoner's dilemma, focusing on minimizing defection via dynamic changes to the payoff matrix. There is no explicit punishment mechanism, no group payoff or efficiency outcome, and the setting is not a public goods game. The findings may be conceptually relevant for understanding how dynamic incentives can shape cooperation, but do not provide quantitative or experimental evidence for the prediction task.

- source: Schimit, P. H. T., Santos, B. O., & Soares, C. A. (2015). Evolution of cooperation in Axelrod tournament using cellular automata. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The study finds that in a spatial evolutionary Prisoner's Dilemma, the success of cooperative strategies depends strongly on the fitness function used for strategy updating. When fitness functions penalize aggressive (high 'caused death') strategies, cooperation rates and the prevalence of cooperative strategies increase. When there is no such penalty, defectors dominate. However, even with punishment-like strategies or fitness penalties, extreme punishment is not always effective at promoting cooperation. Tit-for-tat is not the most successful strategy in this spatial, evolutionary context. The model does not directly measure group efficiency or total group payoff, but higher cooperation is associated with longer average lifetimes (a proxy for individual fitness). The results are not directly transferable to public goods games with explicit costly punishment, but suggest that the structure of incentives for aggression and retaliation can shape cooperation outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides only indirect and adjacent evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The model is a spatial evolutionary Prisoner's Dilemma, not a public goods game, and does not implement explicit costly punishment as a player choice. Instead, some strategies are retaliatory, and some fitness functions penalize aggression. The main outcomes are cooperation rates and strategy prevalence, not group efficiency or total payoff. The findings suggest that penalizing aggression (analogous to punishing defectors) can increase cooperation, but the effect on efficiency is not directly measured. For prediction tasks, this paper may inform the qualitative expectation that punishment or penalties for defection can promote cooperation, but the quantitative effect on efficiency in public goods games cannot be inferred from these results.

- source: Yang, Z. H., & Zhang, L. P. (2023). Random migration with tie retention promotes cooperation in the prisoner's dilemma game. *Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing
  findings: The study finds that tie retention (the ability to keep social ties with ex-neighbors after migration) can facilitate the emergence and maintenance of cooperation in the prisoner's dilemma game, even when migration is random. The maximum number of retained ex-neighbors (m) is a key parameter: higher m leads to higher cooperation rates. Social diversity in tie retention (variation in m across players) further enhances cooperation. Migration probability (p) has a non-monotonic effect: lower p generally favors cooperation, but diversity in p can create optimal conditions for cooperation. The results challenge the previous view that random migration is always detrimental to cooperation. However, all findings are in terms of cooperation rates, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not manipulate or model punishment, nor does it report efficiency, group payoff, or related outcomes. Its main relevance is in showing that network structure, migration, and tie retention can strongly affect cooperation rates in the prisoner's dilemma game. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that social stickiness (tie retention) and migration patterns are important moderators of cooperation, but it does not inform the quantitative effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Palomar, E., Alcaide, A., Ribagorda, A., & Zhang, Y. (2012). The Peer's Dilemma: A general framework to examine cooperation in pure peer-to-peer systems. *Computer Networks*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in a repeated RSP game modeling P2P interactions, the introduction of a 'loner' strategy (voluntary withdrawal) allows for stable coexistence of cooperators, non-cooperators, and loners, preventing the collapse of cooperation even in the absence of incentives or punishment. The system exhibits cyclic dominance of strategies, and the stability of cooperation is sensitive to factors such as population size, network overlay dynamism, and initial population composition. The model does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff as outcomes. Simulations show that large, weakly dynamic populations are more likely to sustain stable cycles, while small or highly dynamic overlays are prone to domination by a single strategy.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public-goods-game-like environments. Its main relevance is in showing that, in a repeated game with voluntary participation (the 'loner' option), stable cooperation can emerge without explicit punishment or reward. The model is adjacent to PGGs but differs in key respects: it is a zero-sum RSP game, not a public goods game, and does not manipulate or analyze punishment, reward, or efficiency. Therefore, it should not be used as direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGGs, but may be useful for understanding the role of voluntary participation and cyclic dominance in sustaining cooperation.

- source: Chen, Q., Pan, Q. H., & He, M. F. (2022). The influence of quasi-cooperative strategy on social dilemma evolution. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that introducing quasi-cooperators (who are intermediate between cooperators and defectors) can, under certain parameter regimes, help maintain cooperation by resisting defectors. However, quasi-cooperators also exploit cooperators, reducing their payoff. The evolutionary outcome depends on parameters such as the temptation to defect, the degree of quasi-cooperation, and the cost to defectors. High costs to defectors and moderate levels of quasi-cooperation can promote the coexistence of cooperators and quasi-cooperators, and suppress defectors. The model does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides only indirect and limited support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. While it discusses how intermediate strategies (quasi-cooperation) can affect the evolutionary stability of cooperation, it does not model or analyze explicit punishment, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff. The findings may be relevant for understanding how intermediate or impure strategies affect cooperation dynamics, but do not provide quantitative or direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency. The model is adjacent to, but not directly informative for, the prediction task.

- source: Tucker, W. T., & Ferson, S. (2008). Evolved altruism, strong reciprocity, and perception of risk. *Strategies for Risk Communication: Evolution, Evidence, Experience*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=N/R | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper argues that human cognitive architecture is shaped by evolutionary pressures to maintain cooperation and punish free riders in social contract networks, such as those modeled by public goods games. It reviews evidence that people are willing to incur personal costs to punish defectors, supporting the concept of 'strong reciprocity.' The authors suggest that these evolved mechanisms are central to risk perception and cooperation, and that they help explain why people often resist narrowly self-interested, rational-maximizing behavior in favor of fairness and contract maintenance. However, the paper does not provide new empirical data or direct analysis of efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and contextual support for the idea that punishment mechanisms are evolutionarily grounded and play a key role in sustaining cooperation in public-goods-like environments. It supports the expectation that punishment can increase cooperation and deter free riding, but does not provide quantitative or direct empirical evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the presence of punishment is likely to increase cooperation and potentially efficiency, but does not provide parameter-specific or design-dimension-specific guidance. Its value is mainly in supporting the plausibility and generality of punishment effects, not in calibrating their magnitude or moderators.

- source: Cheng, H. H., & Meng, X. Z. (2023). Evolution of cooperation in multigame with environmental space and delay. *Biosystems*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds that the evolutionary outcome (dominance or coexistence of cooperation, defection, and environmental space) depends on the mixture of games, the benefit structure of environmental space, and the presence of delay. Large benefits to cooperators from environmental space or reduced benefits to defectors can make pure cooperation the dominant strategy. Large delays can also shift the system from coexistence to pure cooperation. The model does not include explicit punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, but environmental space can function as an indirect punishment by reducing defectors' payoffs. The outcomes are reported as frequencies of strategies, not as group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. It does not manipulate or analyze explicit punishment mechanisms, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. However, it may offer indirect insights into how environmental factors or indirect 'punishment' (via reduced benefits to defectors) can affect the prevalence of cooperation. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is only weakly informative, as it does not address the core dimensions of punishment or efficiency in a directly transferable way.

- source: Lee, S., Murase, Y., & Baek, S. K. (2022). A second-order stability analysis for the continuous model of indirect reciprocity. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that among the leading eight indirect reciprocity norms, only some (notably Simple Standing and L1) are stable against small errors in reputation, meaning that cooperation can recover after perturbations. This stability is linked to how the norm treats justified punishment and cooperation with ill-reputed players. The analysis is entirely theoretical and does not simulate or report group efficiency, payoff, or welfare. The findings are about the mathematical stability of cooperation, not about payoffs or efficiency in a public goods or donation game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. Its results are about the stability of cooperation under different reputation-based norms in indirect reciprocity models, not about payoffs or efficiency. The analysis is adjacent to the prediction task, as it concerns the maintenance of cooperation in the face of errors, but it does not report or model efficiency, group payoff, or surplus. Thus, it should not be used as direct evidence for efficiency prediction, but may provide indirect context about the robustness of cooperation under certain social norms.

- source: Ren, Y. Z., Li, M. C., Xiang, Y., Cui, Y. R., & Sakurai, K. (2013). Evolution of cooperation in reputation system by group-based scheme. *Journal of Supercomputing*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: Simulations show that introducing group competition (group selection) in a repeated Prisoner's Dilemma environment leads to higher cooperation rates and significantly higher average individual payoffs compared to individual selection alone. The effect is robust across different benefit-to-cost ratios and group sizes. Higher benefit-to-cost ratios and smaller group sizes (with constant group number) further enhance cooperation and average payoff. The model does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, but demonstrates that group-based structuring alone can improve efficiency in reputation systems.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that group selection mechanisms (group competition) can substantially increase efficiency (average payoff) in repeated social dilemma environments similar to public goods games, even in the absence of explicit punishment or reward. While it does not directly manipulate or analyze punishment, it is informative for predicting efficiency in designs where group structure or group-level selection is present. The findings suggest that, all else equal, introducing group competition or selection can be expected to increase efficiency relative to individual selection. However, the results do not directly inform the effect of punishment mechanisms, so their predictive value for punishment-enabled vs. control efficiency is limited unless group selection is a relevant design feature.

- source: Madeo, D., & Mocenni, C. (2021). Consensus towards Partially Cooperative Strategies in Self-Regulated Evolutionary Games on Networks. *Games*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that in Stag-Hunt and Chicken games on networks, consensus on partial cooperation can be globally stable and more rewarding (in terms of average payoff) than full cooperation in the Prisoner's Dilemma. The stability of full cooperation, full defection, or partial cooperation depends on the self-regulation parameter relative to network degree. The model does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms; instead, it focuses on endogenous self-regulation. Payoff at equilibrium is analytically and numerically compared across game types and equilibria.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that, in networked games with self-regulation (but without explicit punishment or reward), partial cooperation can be globally stable and yield higher payoffs than full cooperation in the PD. However, because the model does not include punishment, it does not directly inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to enabling punishment in public goods games. The results are most relevant for understanding how internalized social preferences (modeled as self-regulation) affect efficiency, rather than the effect of exogenous punishment mechanisms.

- source: Shen, C., Du, C. P., Mu, C. J., Shia, L., & Wang, Z. (2020). Exit Option Induced by Win-Stay-Lose-Leave Rule Provides Another Route to Solve the Social Dilemma in Structured Populations. *Frontiers in Physics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that allowing players to exit the game (become inactive) according to a win-stay-lose-leave rule, with an associated exit cost, fundamentally alters the dynamics of cooperation in a spatial prisoner's dilemma. When the exit cost is intermediate, inactive players form a buffer at the boundaries of cooperative clusters, protecting cooperators and enabling the expansion of cooperation, which leads to high average payoffs for cooperators and, by implication, higher group welfare. When exit is free (zero cost), too many players become inactive, breaking network reciprocity and causing cooperation and average payoffs to collapse. When exit is very costly, only a few players become inactive, and cooperation can survive but not dominate. The model does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, and the main outcomes are cooperation rate and average payoffs for different strategy types.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it studies a spatial prisoner's dilemma with an exit option and no punishment or reward. However, it provides indirect evidence about how allowing players to opt out (with varying costs) can affect cooperation and average payoffs. The findings suggest that intermediate costs for exit can maximize cooperation and average payoffs, while free exit is detrimental. For the prediction task, this paper is most relevant for understanding the effects of exit/opt-out mechanisms rather than punishment. It does not provide evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency, nor does it manipulate or analyze any of the standard public goods game punishment dimensions.

- source: Xu, Z. J., Zhi, H. Z., & Zhang, L. Z. (2011). Survival via cooperation in the prisoner's dilemma game. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The study finds that in a spatial prisoner's dilemma with ecological dynamics, individuals are more likely to cooperate in highly competitive environments (high survival cost), and the average level of cooperation increases as the temptation to defect decreases. Clusters of cooperators can form and persist, especially when survival costs are low. The model does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report on efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. It models a spatial prisoner's dilemma with ecological dynamics, focusing on cooperation rates and strategy survival, not on payoff or efficiency. Punishment is only mentioned as a possible mechanism in the discussion, not implemented or analyzed. Therefore, its relevance for predicting efficiency effects of punishment is very limited.

- source: Xu, M., Zheng, D. F., Xu, C., & Hui, P. M. (2017). Three-strategy N-person snowdrift game incorporating loners. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a loner strategy into the N-person snowdrift game leads to three possible steady states: all cooperators (AllC), all loners (AllL), or a mixed state of cooperators and defectors (C+D). The AllC state, which corresponds to maximum efficiency, is only possible in a very small region of parameter space (low cost/benefit ratio r and high loner payoff L). Most of the parameter space results in either a C+D state (intermediate efficiency) or an AllL state (lower efficiency, as loners receive a fixed, suboptimal payoff). The presence of loners can help eliminate defectors in the transient, enabling the possibility of an AllC state, but this is rare. The model does not include punishment or reward mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants. However, it does provide informative theoretical results about how the availability of an outside option (loner strategy) affects group efficiency and the conditions under which full cooperation can be achieved. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is not directly informative about punishment effects, but it does highlight that the structure of available strategies (e.g., presence of loners) and the payoff parameters can strongly affect efficiency outcomes. The absence of punishment or reward means it cannot be used to predict the effect of enabling punishment, but it may be useful for understanding baseline efficiency in adjacent game structures.

- source: Fort, H. (2003). Exploring the cooperative regimes in an agent-based model: indirect reciprocity vs. selfish incentives. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that, in a large population of memoryless, tagless agents playing repeated random pairwise Prisoner's Dilemma games, the system self-organizes into stationary states with positive average cooperation and per capita income, even in the absence of direct reciprocity, punishment, or reward. The equilibrium level of cooperation and group payoff depend strongly on the specific payoff matrix used. Some payoff matrices that intuitively seem to favor cooperation actually result in low cooperation and low group payoff, due to agents' expectations and update rules. The model demonstrates robustness of cooperation to changes in the payoff matrix, but does not include any mechanism for punishment, sanctioning, or reward.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment or sanctions on efficiency or group payoff in public-goods-like games. It is useful as a baseline for understanding how efficiency and cooperation can emerge in the absence of punishment, but does not inform the prediction of treatment efficiency when punishment is enabled. The findings may be useful for modeling control conditions (no punishment) in related agent-based or repeated PD environments, but cannot be used to estimate the effect of punishment or its moderators.

- source: Li, C., Xu, H. D., & Fan, S. H. (2020). Synergistic effects of self-optimization and imitation rules on the evolution of cooperation in the investor sharing game. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that the proposed dynamic strategy updating rule can substantially increase the density of cooperators in the investor sharing game, especially at low aspiration levels. The self-optimization rule increases the probability that defectors with high investment become cooperators, which then promotes further imitation of cooperation. Lower market monopoly (parameter alpha) and more random network structure also boost cooperation, but the effect of network randomness is stronger. Average payoffs for cooperators can exceed those for defectors at high cooperation rates, but group efficiency is not directly analyzed. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not inform prediction of punishment effects on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. Its findings on cooperation rates and average payoffs under different strategy updating rules and aspiration levels may be of indirect interest for understanding how endogenous learning rules affect cooperation, but it does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment, nor does it report group efficiency as defined for the prediction task.

- source: Li, B., Zhao, X. W., & Xia, H. X. (2019). Promotion of cooperation by Hybrid Migration mechanisms in the Spatial Prisoner's Dilemma Game. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that mixing success-driven migration with mean-payoff-driven or escaping-defector-driven migration (hybrid migration mechanisms) can significantly increase the fraction of cooperators in a spatial Prisoner's Dilemma game, especially under certain population densities and mixture levels. In some cases, these mechanisms also lead to higher average payoffs for individuals. The effect is sensitive to the mixture parameter and population density. No punishment or sanctioning is modeled, and the main focus is on cooperation rates rather than efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. It does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, and the main outcomes are cooperation rates and, to a lesser extent, average payoff in a spatial Prisoner's Dilemma context. The findings may be of limited indirect value for understanding how migration or mobility affects cooperation, but they do not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment in public goods games.

  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  findings: The study finds that in the N-person chicken game, the final proportion of cooperators depends sensitively and sometimes non-monotonically on the payoff function parameters (R and S). For certain parameter ranges, small changes can lead to drastic shifts in cooperation rates. The system stabilizes to a constant or oscillating cooperation rate, but the group efficiency or total payoff is not directly analyzed. No punishment, reward, or sanctioning mechanisms are included in the model.

However, it does provide insight into how the structure of payoff functions (e.g., the relative rewards for cooperation and defection) can lead to abrupt changes in cooperation rates in N-person social dilemmas. - source: Zhang, C. Y., Li, Q. Y., Zhu, Y. Y., Han, J. D., & Zhang, J. L. (2020). Evolutionary dynamics in division of labor games on cycle networks. *European Journal of Control*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing
  findings: The study finds that in division of labor games on cycle networks, the update rule (BD, DB, or mixed) has a major impact on the speed at which efficient strategies (interpreted as high-quality task allocation) spread through the population. Under strong selection, BD updating leads to fixation times that scale linearly with population size, while DB updating leads to quadratic or cubic scaling, meaning much slower convergence. The mixed rule interpolates between these extremes. The fixation probability and time depend on the payoff matrix parameters, but the presence or absence of punishment is not considered. The results are relevant for understanding how structural and dynamic features of the game affect the emergence of efficient collective behavior, but do not directly address efficiency as a payoff ratio or the effect of punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants, as it does not include any punishment or sanction mechanism. However, it does offer indirect insight into how update rules and network structure can affect the speed and likelihood of efficient collective behavior emerging in games with a cooperative dilemma structure. For prediction tasks focused on punishment effects, this paper is not directly informative, but it may be useful for understanding baseline dynamics in the absence of punishment and for considering the role of update mechanisms and network topology.

- source: Podobnik, B., Jusup, M., Wang, Z., & Stanley, H. E. (2017). How Fear of Future Outcomes Affects Social Dynamics. *Journal of Statistical Physics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that the stability of benevolence (and thus group-level efficiency or welfare) in an open society depends on the interplay between insiders' tolerance, the assimilation rate of outsiders, and the network structure of interactions. As the fraction of outsiders increases, benevolence (measured by the fitness ratio R) generally decreases. If insiders' tolerance is too low or assimilation is too slow, the system can experience a sudden breakdown of cooperation (herd behavior), leading to antagonism and loss of mutual benefit. If tolerance and assimilation are balanced, a stable mutualistic equilibrium can be maintained. The model does not include punishment or sanctioning, so it does not inform the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. However, it does provide indirect insight into how the withdrawal of benevolence (analogous to defection or exclusion) can destabilize cooperation and reduce group welfare. The model's outcomes are determined by tolerance and assimilation parameters, not by punishment or reward. Therefore, this paper should not be used as direct evidence for the prediction task focused on punishment effects.

- source: Geng, Y. N., Lu, Y. K., Hong, L. J., & Shi, L. (2025). Evolution of cooperation with asymmetric rewards. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that asymmetric rewards in a two-population prisoner's dilemma model can sustain cooperation and prevent total defection, especially in structured populations (lattices). The effect of asymmetry on cooperation depends on the dilemma strength: under strong dilemma (high temptation to defect), asymmetry promotes cooperation; under weak dilemma, strong asymmetry can actually inhibit cooperation. The model includes both prosocial and antisocial rewards but does not include punishment or sanctions. The outcomes are reported in terms of cooperation rates and the coexistence of different strategies, not in terms of efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as it does not study punishment, public goods games, or efficiency/welfare outcomes. Its findings on asymmetric rewards in the prisoner's dilemma may be of limited indirect relevance for understanding how positive incentives (rewards) can sustain cooperation, but it does not inform the prediction task regarding punishment or efficiency. The game structure, outcome measures, and intervention type (reward, not punishment) are all different from the target prediction context.

- source: Liu, Y. F., Geng, Y. N., Du, C. P., Hu, K. P., Shen, C., Pansini, R., & Shi, L. (2021). The interface of unidirectional rewards: Enhanced cooperation within interdependent networks. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent

  findings: The study finds that unidirectional, asymmetric reward from an upper to a lower network layer can sustain or enhance cooperation in both layers, depending on the temptation to defect and the reward factor. When only pro-social reward is allowed, defectors tend to dominate; when only anti-social reward is allowed, cooperators can dominate. The model does not include punishment or negative sanctions, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. The results are based on simulations and theoretical analysis of cooperation rates, not on empirical or experimental data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It models a prisoner's dilemma with unidirectional reward (not punishment) in a networked setting, and reports only on cooperation rates, not efficiency or payoff. The findings may be indirectly relevant for understanding how positive incentives (rewards) affect cooperation, but do not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment. The game structure is adjacent to, but not the same as, a public goods game, and the outcome measures are not payoff-based.

- source: Powers, S. T., Ekárt, A., & Lewis, P. R. (2018). Modelling enduring institutions: The complementarity of evolutionary and agent-based approaches. *Cognitive Systems Research*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that participants' choices in one-shot Prisoner's Dilemma games are influenced by information about the other player's action and by individual personality traits. The disjunction effect (greater cooperation under uncertainty) is present but weaker than in previous studies and is not explained by a general bias to cooperate. Higher potential payoffs (high variability matrices) reduce the disjunction effect, suggesting more optimal responding when incentives are larger. Reward Responsiveness predicts more optimal (defecting) choices when the optimal strategy is to defect and the other player's action is known. Agreeableness predicts more cooperative choices when the optimal strategy is to cooperate. No evidence is found for a general bias toward cooperation, and the results are similar whether the task is framed as a social dilemma or a neutral card game. The study does not include punishment, sanctions, or efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. It does not include punishment or sanctions, nor does it analyze efficiency, group payoff, or related payoff-based outcomes. Its findings on the influence of payoff variability and personality on cooperation/defection may be contextually informative for understanding individual decision-making in social dilemmas, but it does not inform the downstream prediction task regarding punishment effects or efficiency.

The main reported outcome is the fraction of cooperators, not efficiency or group payoff. No punishment or reward mechanisms are included. The findings suggest that introspective learning (Q-learning) can promote cooperation more effectively than imitation dynamics, but the results are not directly transferable to efficiency or payoff-based outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. It does not include punishment, reward, or sanctioning mechanisms, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. Its findings on cooperation rates under Q-learning may be contextually informative for understanding learning dynamics, but are not directly relevant for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency.

  findings: The study finds that allowing cooperators to form 'benefit communities'—groups that share their payoffs equally—substantially increases the fraction of cooperators in the population, especially on small-world networks compared to regular lattices. The mechanism is more effective at lower temptations to defect (b), and its effect diminishes as b increases. The main outcome is the stationary fraction of cooperators, not group payoff or efficiency. No punishment, sanctioning, or reward mechanisms are present or analyzed.

Its findings about the effect of a 'benefit community' mechanism on cooperation in the prisoner's dilemma may be of limited conceptual interest for understanding how group-benefit mechanisms can promote cooperation, but it does not inform the prediction task regarding punishment or efficiency in PGG-like games.

- source: Gao, H. Y., Wang, J., Zhang, F., Li, X. P., & Xia, C. Y. (2022). Evolution of cooperation under the aspiration-based interactive diversity in the spatial prisoner's dilemma game. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The study finds that in a spatial prisoner's dilemma game where players can adopt different strategies toward each neighbor (interactive diversity) and update their strategies based on an aspiration threshold, the fraction of cooperation (F_c) can be significantly increased by tuning the aspiration parameter (alpha). There is an optimal range of aspiration for each level of temptation to defect (b), and too high or too low aspiration can suppress cooperation.   findings: The introduction of two-sided players into the iterated prisoner's dilemma on networks leads to a substantial reduction in the fraction of defectors and promotes cooperation, especially as the initial fraction of two-sided players increases. The study tracks the evolution of strategy frequencies but does not report on group payoff, efficiency, or welfare. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present.

- source: Lu, P. (2015). Imitating winner or sympathizing loser? Quadratic effects on cooperative behavior in prisoners' dilemma games. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  findings: The paper finds that in a spatial prisoners' dilemma, the probability of imitating losers ('sympathy') and the temptation to defect both have quadratic effects on the average cooperation rate. For low values of sympathy, increasing it reduces cooperation, but above a threshold, further increases in sympathy promote cooperation. Temptation similarly has a threshold effect. The model does not include punishment, reward, or any sanctioning mechanism, and does not report efficiency or payoff-based outcomes. The findings are about the dynamics of cooperation rates under different imitation rules.

- source: Sun, L., Lin, P. J., & Chen, Y. S. (2014). Voluntary strategy suppresses the positive impact of preferential selection in prisoner's dilemma. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that preferential selection (aspiring to the most successful neighbor, i.e., positive w) promotes cooperation in the voluntary PDG for low temptation to defect (small b), but this effect is suppressed as b increases. For negative w (aspiring to less successful neighbors), cooperation is suppressed and all three strategies (cooperator, defector, loner) can coexist. The voluntary participation mechanism (the loner strategy) changes the impact of preferential selection compared to standard PDG. However, the paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, so its findings are limited to behavioral outcomes (strategy frequencies).
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as it does not include punishment or analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes. Its findings are only indirectly relevant, as they show how voluntary participation and preferential imitation affect cooperation rates in a related but distinct game (voluntary PDG). It does not inform the prediction task regarding punishment effects or efficiency outcomes.

- source: Li, S. L., Hong, L. J., Geng, Y. N., & Shen, C. (2020). Popularity-driven fitness calculation promotes cooperation in spatial prisoner's dilemma game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The study finds that integrating popularity into the fitness function (via a tunable parameter alpha) in a spatial prisoner's dilemma game can significantly promote cooperation. When alpha is positive, popular individuals (those whose neighbors share their strategy) gain higher fitness, leading to the formation and expansion of cooperative clusters and, in some cases, full cooperation. When alpha is negative, isolated cooperators can persist but do not dominate. The results are supported by both numerical simulations and mean-field theoretical analysis. However, the paper does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or any payoff-based outcome, nor does it include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games, as it does not include punishment, reward, or efficiency outcomes. Its findings are only indirectly relevant, suggesting that mechanisms which enhance the fitness of popular strategies can promote cooperation, but this is not directly transferable to the efficiency effects of punishment. The game studied is a spatial prisoner's dilemma, not a public goods game, and the outcome of interest is the fraction of cooperators, not group payoff or efficiency.

- source: Zhang, M., Wang, S. Y., Hu, X. T., & Alfaro-Bittner, K. (2021). Local and global information affect cooperation in networked Prisoner's dilemma games. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment

  findings: The study finds that providing players with global information about their opponents' strategies, or allowing them to choose between local and global information, increases the frequency of cooperation compared to providing only local information. In the local information condition, cooperation declines steadily over time. In the global and discretionary conditions, some sessions show a reversal with increasing cooperation in later rounds. Players strongly prefer global information when given a choice. The experiment does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.

However, since efficiency or payoff outcomes are not analyzed, and punishment is not present, this paper offers only weak, contextual support for the prediction task.

  findings: The paper finds that, under weak selection, the self-referential updating rule (where players are more likely to abandon their strategy if dissatisfied with their payoff) leads to a greater difference in the stationary frequencies of favored versus unfavored strategies than the standard Moran process.
